这一声之后，楼下顿时骚动起来；呼喝阵阵，笑闹绵延；一片嘈杂的鼎沸，里头单个的声响再也分不出来。斯托滕贝格的神经一根根发麻。到这时他已十拿九稳，认出了那副压不住的快活嗓音。有身份的绅士！瞎子！逗乐！可是，倘若尼古拉斯对昨日之事的回报不假，这人已是个不折不扣的废人了。他自己既然这般全然无助、这般狼狈，又是什么引得他把脑袋送进豺狼口中，来冲撞他得胜的仇敌呢？

斯托滕贝格勋爵悬着的心没有悬多久。就在他俯望那群兴高采烈、情绪沸腾的人时，他已能在人堆正中分辨出一副宽肩膀——那肩膀只可能属于一个人。士兵们大笑着，把这场嬉闹当作十足的享受，你推我搡地挤兑他，只图跟这样一条魁梧大汉较量较量胆气的快活。而他呢，尽管瞎了，却半点不让人；拳脚并用，骁勇应战，给了捉弄他的人好几下重击；直到有几个人欢叫一声，合力把他抱住，扛上肩头，抬进了松脂火把围出的一圈光亮里。

回应他的是一声两下里的惊呼——斯托滕贝格的一声诧异，尼古拉斯的一声惊骇。但两人都没有作声。斯托滕贝格的双唇抿得紧紧的；眉心蹙起一道困惑的褶子。说实话，在他这恶魔生涯里破天荒一遭，他全然措手不及，不知如何是好。他眼前所见的那个人，衣衫褴褛，胡子不剃，满身泥污，一双失明的眼睛眨巴着——正是他在这世上厌恶到极点、胜过世间万事万物的那个人——往日那个天不怕地不怕的雇佣冒险家，一度是骄傲而得手的情敌；如今只是一具残破的人形，垮了，是啊，还堕落了，从今往后只是一桩惹人怜悯的物事，再难威胁他情敌的筹谋。他的紧身上衣成了破布，插羽的帽子瘪塌塌的，脚趾从靴子的破洞里露出来。他的上半张脸缠着一条脏了的亚麻绷带。那绷带起初无疑是想护住受伤的眼睛的；可它已经滑了下来，正是那双眼睛，带着那种可怕的定定的神情，在摇曳的火光里闪出一种非人间的诡谲。

===Original===
“Salute his Magnificence, the lord and master of Amersfoort and of all that in it lies!” one of the soldiers shouted gaily.

And the blind man forthwith made a gesture of obeisance swept with a wide flourish his battered plumed hat from off his head.

“To his Magnificence!” he called out in response. “Though mine eyes cannot see him, my voice is raised in praise of his nobility and his valour. May the recording angels give him his full deserts.”

===Chinese===
「向大人阁下致敬——阿默斯福特的主宰，城里一切生灵的主子！」一个士兵兴高采烈地喊道。

盲人当即做了一个致敬的姿势，潇洒地一挥，把那顶瘪塌塌的插羽帽子从头上抹了下来。

「敬大人阁下！」他扬声应道。「尽管我这双眼睛见不着他，我这条嗓子可要放开了替他歌功颂德，赞他的高贵，赞他的英勇。但愿记功的天使们给他记足他应得的那份报偿。」

### III / 三

===Original===
The feeling of sheer horror which had caused Nicolaes to utter a sudden cry was, in truth, fully justified.

“It can’t be!” he murmured, appalled at what he saw.

Stoutenburg answered with a hoarse laugh. “Nay, by Satan and all his myrmidons it is!”

Already he was leaning out of the window, giving quick orders to the men down below to bring that drunken vagabond forthwith into his presence. After which he turned once more to his friend.

===Chinese===
方才促使尼古拉斯脱口惊呼的那股纯粹的恐惧，说实话，是全然有由的。

「不可能是他！」他喃喃道，被眼前的景象吓坏了。

斯托滕贝格用一声沙哑的大笑作答。「不，凭撒旦和他满门的爪牙，就是他！」

他已经探身窗外，急促地下令，叫底下的人即刻把那个醉醺醺的流浪汉带到他跟前来。说完又回身对着他的朋友。

===Original===
“We’ll soon see,” he said, “if it is true, or if our eyes have played us both an elusive trick. Yet, methinks,” he added thoughtfully, “that the pigwidgeon who of late hath taken my destiny in hand is apparently intent on doing me a good turn.”

“In what way?” the other asked.

“By throwing my enemy across my path,” Stoutenburg replied drily.

“You’ll hang him of course?” Nicolaes rejoined.

“Yes; I’ll hang him!” Stoutenburg retorted, with a snarl. “But I must make use of him first.”

===Chinese===
「咱们很快就见分晓，」他说，「到底是真，还是咱们两人的眼睛都使了同一个捉弄人的花招。不过我看，」他若有所思地补了一句，「那个近来把我命运攥在掌心的小妖精，眼下分明是一心要帮我一个忙呢。」

「怎么讲？」对方问。

「他把我的仇家径直摔到了我的路上来，」斯托滕贝格干巴巴地答道。

「你自然要吊死他了？」尼古拉斯应道。

「对；我要吊死他！」斯托滕贝格咆哮着回道。「不过先得拿他派派用场。」

===Original===
“Make use of him? How?”

“That I do not know as yet. But inspiration will come, never you fear, my friend. All that I want is a leverage for bringing the Stadtholder to his knees and for winning Gilda’s love.”

“Then, in Heaven’s name, man,” Nicolaes rejoined earnestly, “begin by ridding yourself of the only danger-spoke in your wheel!”

“Danger-spoke?” Stoutenburg exclaimed, threw back his head and laughed. “Would you really call that miserable oaf a serious bar to mine ambition or a possible rival in your sister’s regard?”

===Chinese===
「派用场？怎么派？」

「这我眼下还不知道。可灵感会来的，我的朋友，你尽管放心。我要的不过是一根杠杆，好叫执政（Stadtholder）屈膝，好赢得吉尔达的爱。」

「既然如此，看在老天的份上，伙计，」尼古拉斯恳切地应道，「那就先把你车轮上唯一那根闯祸的辐条拔了吧！」

「闯祸的辐条？」斯托滕贝格一仰头大笑。「你当真把那个可怜的蠢汉当成一回事，算他是我雄图上的一道严重障碍，或是你妹妹心中一个堪虑的情敌么？」

===Original===
And, with outstretched hand he pointed to the door.

There, under the lintel—pushed on by Jan and two or three men who, powerfully built though they were, looked like pygmies beside the stricken giant, drunk as an owl, his hat awry above that hideous bandage, dirty, unkempt, and ragged—appeared the man who had once been the brilliant inspiration of Franz Hals’ immortal *Laughing Cavalier*.

At sight of him Nicolaes Beresteyn gave a loud groan and collapsed into a chair; burying his face in his hand. He was ever a coward, even in villainy; and when the man whom he had once hated so bitterly, and whom his craven hand had struck in such a dastardly manner, lurched into the room, and as he fell against the table uttered an inane and bibulous laugh, his nerve completely forsook him.

===Chinese===
说着，他伸手指向房门。

就在那里，门楣底下——扬和两三条大汉推搡着他往前走；那几条汉子虽则筋骨雄壮，立在这个遭难的巨人身边却像一群侏儒——他烂醉如泥，帽子歪戴在那条可怕的绷带上方，肮脏、蓬乱、褴褛——来的这个人，正是当年以翩翩英姿激发了弗兰斯·哈尔斯（Frans Hals）那幅不朽名作《笑面骑士》（*The Laughing Cavalier*）灵感的那个人。

一看见他，尼古拉斯·贝雷斯坦（Nicolaes Beresteyn）便大声呻吟一声，瘫倒在椅子里，把脸埋进手中。他向来是个懦夫，连作恶都透着怯懦；当他曾经恨之入骨、又被他那只怯懦的手卑鄙地暗算过的人踉跄着闯进屋来、又倚倒在桌边发出一声痴傻的醉笑时，他的胆气彻底弃他而去了。

===Original===
At a peremptory sign from Stoutenburg, Jan closed the doors which gave on the hall; but he and two of the men remained at attention inside the room.

The blind man groped with his hands till they found a chair, into which he sank, with powerful limbs outstretched, snorting like a dog just come out of the water. With an awkward gesture he pushed his hat from off his head, and in so doing he dislodged the grimy bandage so that it sat like a scullion’s cap across his white forehead.

Stoutenburg watched him with an expression of cruel satisfaction. It is not often given to a man to have an enemy and a rival so completely in his power, and the exultation in Stoutenburg’s heart was so great that he was content to savour it in silence for awhile. Nicolaes was beyond the power of speech, and so the silence for a moment or two remained absolute.

===Chinese===
斯托滕贝格专断地一示意，扬便关上了通向大厅的门；但他和另外两名汉子仍留在房内肃立侍命。

盲人双手摸索着，摸到了一把椅子，便一屁股陷了进去，有力的四肢摊开着，像一条刚从水里爬出来的狗似的喷着鼻息。他笨拙地一挥手，把帽子从头上抹掉；这一抹带歪了那条污秽的绷带，叫它像一顶厨役的便帽似的横在他白皙的额头上。

斯托滕贝格带着一种残忍的满足望着他。一个人能将仇敌兼情敌这般彻底地攥在掌心，这等际遇世人难得碰上；斯托滕贝格心中的得意如此盛大，以致他甘愿先在沉默里细细品味一番。尼古拉斯则已失了言语的能力，于是那一两分钟的沉默，成了十足的沉默。

===Original===
Then the blind man suddenly sat up, craning his neck and rolling his sightless eyes.

“I wonder where the devil I am!” he murmured through set lips. He appeared to listen intently; no doubt caught the sound of life around him, for he added quickly: “Is anybody here?”

“I am here,” Stoutenburg replied curtly. “Do you know whom I am, sirrah?”

“In truth, I do not,” Diogenes replied. “But by your accent I would judge you to be a man who at this moment is mightily afraid.”

===Chinese===
随后盲人忽然坐直了身子，伸着脖颈，滚动着那双失明的眼睛。

「我倒纳闷，我这到底是在什么鬼地方！」他从抿紧的唇缝里喃喃道。他像是在凝神细听；无疑捕捉到了周遭的人声动静，因为他紧接着补问：「这里有人吗？」

「我在这里，」斯托滕贝格简截地答道。「你可知道你眼前的是什么人，家伙？」

「说实话，不知道，」第欧根尼（Diogenes）答道。「不过听您的口音，我敢断定，您此刻正怕得厉害呢。」

===Original===
“Afraid?” Stoutenburg retorted, with a loud laugh. “I, afraid of a helpless vagabond who has been fool enough to run his head into a noose which I had not even thought of preparing for him?”

“Yet you are afraid my lord,” the other rejoined quietly, “else you would not have ordered your bodyguard to watch over your precious person whilst you parleyed with a blind man.”

“My bodyguard is only waiting for final orders to take you to the gallows,” Stoutenburg rejoined roughly. “You may as well know now as later that it is my intention to hang you.”

“As well now as later,” the blind man assented, with easy philosophy. “I understand that for the nonce, whoever you Magnificence may be, you are master in Amersfoort. As such, you have a right to hang anyone you choose. Me or another. What matters? I was very nearly hung once, you must know, by the Lord of Stoutenburg. I did not mind much then; I’d mind it still less now. People talk of a hereafter. Well, whatever it is, it must be a better world that this, so I would just as soon as not, go and find out for myself.”

===Chinese===
「怕？」斯托滕贝格大笑一声，反唇相讥。「我，怕一个无助的流浪汉？这蠢货蠢到自个儿把脑袋往绳套里钻，那绳套我压根还没想着给他备下呢。」

「可您就是在害怕，勋爵大人，」对方静静地应道，「不然您也不会吩咐您的卫兵守着您宝贵的贵体，来跟一个瞎子会谈了。」

「我的卫兵只等一声最后的号令，送你上绞架，」斯托滕贝格粗声应道。「晚知道不如早知道：我打算吊死你。」

「早知道晚知道都一样，」盲人带着一身从容的哲人气度应道。「我明白了，眼下不管您这位大人阁下是何方神圣，您总是阿默斯福特的主子。既是主子，您自然有权吊死任何一个您看中的人。吊我也好，吊别人也好。有什么打紧？您可得知道，我有一回差点叫斯托滕贝格勋爵给吊死。那会儿我就没怎么在意；如今就更不在乎了。人们总说什么来世。哼，管它是什么，总得比这个世界强些，所以趁便去亲眼瞧个明白，我倒也无所谓。」

===Original===
He struggled to his feet, still groping with his hands for support, found the edge of the table and leaned up against it.

“Let’s to the hangman, my lord,” he said thickly. “If I’m to hang, I prefer it to be done at once. And if we tarry too long I might get sober ere I embark on the last adventure. But,” he added, and once more appeared to search the room with eyes that could not see, “there’s someone else here besides your lordship. Who is it?”

“My friend and yours,” Stoutenburg replied. “Mynheer Nicolaes Beresteyn.”

There was a second or two of silence. Nicolaes made as if he would speak, but Stoutenburg quickly put a finger up to his lips, enjoining him to remain still. The blind man passed his trembling hand once or twice in front of his eyes as if to draw aside an unseen veil that hid the outer world from his gaze.

“Ah!” he murmured contentedly. “My friend Klaas! He is here too, is he? That is indeed good news. For Nicolaes was ever my friend. That time three months ago—or was it three years, or three centuries? I really have lost count—that time that the Lord of Stoutenburg was on the point of hanging me, Klaas would have interposed on my behalf, only something went wrong with his heart at the moment, or his nerves, I forget which.”

===Chinese===
他挣扎着站起来，双手仍旧摸索着寻撑扶，摸到了桌沿，便倚在上面。

「这就去见刽子手吧，勋爵大人，」他口齿含混地说。「既然要吊死我，我宁可趁早办了。咱们若是磨蹭太久，只怕我踏上最后这场冒险之前，酒就醒过来了。不过，」他添了一句，又像要用那双看不见的眼睛把屋子搜索一遍，「这里除了勋爵大人您，还有旁人。是谁？」

「我的朋友，也是你的朋友，」斯托滕贝格答道。「尼古拉斯·贝雷斯坦老爷（Mynheer）。」

有那么一两秒钟的沉默。尼古拉斯像要开口，斯托滕贝格却迅速竖起一根手指抵在唇上，命他不要作声。盲人把颤抖的手在眼睛前挥了一两下，像是要撩开一幅遮断外界、挡住他目光的无形帷幕。

「啊！」他心满意足地喃喃道。「我的朋友克拉斯（Klaas）！他也在这儿，是么？这可真是天大的好消息。要知道尼古拉斯从来是我的朋友。三个月前那回——还是三年，还是三个世纪来着？我实在记不清了——那一回斯托滕贝格勋爵正要把我吊上架子，克拉斯本是要替我求情的，只可惜那一刻他的心脏——还是神经来着——出了点岔子，我忘了是哪一样。」

===Original===
“ ’Twere no use to rely on mynheer’s interference this time,” Stoutenburg put in drily. “There is but one person in the world now who can save you from the gallows.”

“You mean the Lord of Stoutenburg himself?” the blind man queried blandly.

“Nay! He is determined to hang you. But there is another.”

“Then I pray your lordship to tell me who that other is,” Diogenes replied.

“You might find one, sirrah, in the jongejuffrouw Gilda Beresteyn, the Lord of Stoutenburg’s promised wife.”

===Chinese===
「这一回，指望这位老爷打圆场可没用了，」斯托滕贝格干巴巴地插进来说。「如今普天之下，只有一个人能救你不落绞架。」

「您是说斯托滕贝格勋爵本人？」盲人不紧不慢地问道。

「不！他是铁了心要吊死你。不过另有其人。」

「那我就恳请勋爵大人示下，那另一位是谁，」第欧根尼答道。

「这另一个么，小子，你不妨在贝雷斯坦家的吉尔达小姐（jongejuffrouw）身上去找——她可是斯托滕贝格勋爵的未婚妻。」

===Original===
Diogenes made no reply to this. He was facing the table now, still clinging to it with one hand, whilst the other wandered over the objects on the table. Suddenly they encountered a crystal jug which was full of wine. An expression of serene beatitude overspread his face. He raised the goblet to his lips, but ere he drank he said carelessly:

“Ah, the jongejuffrouw Beresteyn is the promised wife of the Lord of Stoutenburg?”

“My promised wife!” Stoutenburg put in roughly. “Methought you would ere this have recognized the man whom you tried to rob of all that he held most precious.”

“Your lordship must forgive me,” the blind man rejoined drily. “But some unknown miscreant—whom may the gods punish—interfered with me yesterday forenoon, when I was trying to render assistance to my friend Klaas. In the scuffle that ensued, I received a cloud of stinking fumes in the face, which has totally robbed me of sight.”

===Chinese===
第欧根尼对此没有作答。他此刻正对着桌子，一只手仍攀着桌沿，另一只手却在桌上的物件间游走。忽然，那手碰上了一只斟满酒的水晶壶。一种恬然的至福漫上了他的脸。他举起杯子凑向唇边，可在喝之前，他漫不经心地说：

「哦，贝雷斯坦家的小姐是斯托滕贝格勋爵的未婚妻？」

「我的未婚妻！」斯托滕贝格粗声插道。「我还当事到如今，你总该认出这个你妄想夺走他最珍爱之物的人了。」

「勋爵大人务必恕罪，」盲人干巴巴地应道。「只是昨天上午，我正想给我朋友克拉斯搭把手的时候，有个来历不明的恶徒——愿众神惩罚他——横插了一杠子。随后那场混战里，我脸上挨了一团恶臭的毒烟，就把我的视力整个儿夺走了。」

===Original===
As he spoke he raised his eyes, blinking in that pathetic and inconsequent manner peculiar to the blind. Nicolaes gave an audible groan. He could not bear to look on those sightless orbs, which in the flickering light of the wax candles appeared weird and unearthly.

“Oh,” Stoutenburg put in carelessly, “is that how the—er—accident occurred?”

“So, please your lordship, yes,” Diogenes replied. “And I was left stranded on the moor, since those two unreclaimed varlets, Pythagoras and Socrates by name, did effectually ride off in the wake of the Stadtholder, leaving me in the lurch. A pitiable plight, your lordship will admit.”

“So pitiable,” the other retorted with a sneer, “that you thought to improve your condition by bearding the Lord of Stoutenburg in his lair.”

===Chinese===
说话间他抬起眼睛，用盲人特有的那种凄惶而漫无着落的方式眨巴着。尼古拉斯出声地呻吟了一下。他不忍看那双失明的眼珠——在蜡炬摇曳的光里，它们显得诡谲而不似人间之物。

「哦，」斯托滕贝格漫不经心地插话，「那桩——呃——意外，就是这么来的？」

「回勋爵大人的话，正是如此，」第欧根尼答道。「我就那样被撇在荒原上进退不得，因为那两个不可救药的下贱坯——一个叫毕达哥拉斯（Pythagoras），一个叫苏格拉底（Socrates）——倒是脚底抹油，跟着执政一溜烟走了，把我撂在了半道。勋爵大人想必承认，这光景够凄惨的。」

「凄惨到这个地步，」对方冷笑着反诘，「以致你想出个法子来改一改处境——摸进斯托滕贝格勋爵的虎穴里去捋他的虎须。」

===Original===
“I did not know your lordship was in Amersfoort,” Diogenes replied imperturbably. “I thought—I hoped—”

He paused, and Stoutenburg tried in vain to read what went on behind that seemingly unclouded brow. The blind man appeared serene, detached, perfectly good-humoured. His slender hand, which looked hard beneath its coating of grime, was closed lovingly around the crystal jug. Stoutenburg vaguely wondered how far the man was really drunk, or whether his misfortune had slightly addled his brain. So much unconcern in the face of an imminent and shameful death gave an uncanny air to the whole appearance of the man. Even now, with a gently apologetic smile, he raised the jug once more to his lips. Stoutenburg placed a peremptory hand upon his arm.

“Put that down, man,” he said harshly. “You are drunk enough as it is, and you’ll have need of all your wits tonight.”

“There you are wrong my lord,” Diogenes retorted, and quietly transferred the jug to his other hand. “A man, meseems, needs no wits to hang gracefully. And I feel that I could do that best if I might quench my thirst ere I met my friend the hangman.”

===Chinese===
「我并不知道勋爵大人您在阿默斯福特，」第欧根尼不动声色地答道。「我以为——我盼着——」

他顿住了；斯托滕贝格想看透那似乎毫无阴云的额头底下转着什么念头，却是徒劳。盲人显得安详、超然、心情极佳。他那只修长的手——泥污底下透着粗硬——正宝贝似的拢着那只水晶酒壶。斯托滕贝格隐隐纳闷，这人到底醉到了几分，又或者他的厄运把他的脑子搅坏了些许。面对迫在眉睫的、身败名裂的死，竟有这么多的满不在乎，给这人的整个仪表添上一层说不出的诡异。便是此刻，他还带着一个温存的、告罪似的微笑，又一次把酒壶举向唇边。斯托滕贝格一只专断的手按上了他的手臂。

「把那玩意儿放下，伙计，」他狠狠地说。「你已经醉得可以了，今夜你那点脑子怕是还大有用处。」

「这您可就说错了，勋爵大人，」第欧根尼回嘴道，一边不动声色地把酒壶换到另一只手里。「依我看，一个人要吊得体面，原用不着什么脑子。而且我总觉得，若能在见我那位刽子手朋友之前解了渴，这桩事我准能办得最漂亮。」

===Original===
“You may not meet him at all.”

“But just now you said—”

“That it was my intention to hang you,” Stoutenburg assented. “So it is. But I am in rare good humour tonight, and—”

“So it seems, my lord,” the blind man put in carelessly. “So it seems.”

===Chinese===
「你也可能压根见不着他。」

「可您方才还说——」

「说我打算吊死你，」斯托滕贝格认了。「正是。不过我今夜兴致难得地好，而且——」

「看得出，勋爵大人，」盲人漫不经心地插了一句。「看得出。」

===Original===
He appeared to be swaying on his feet, and to have some difficulty in retaining his balance. He still clung to the edge of the table with one hand. In the other he had the jug fill of wine.

“The jongejuffrouw Gilda Beresteyn,” Stoutenburg went on, “will sup with me this night to celebrate our betrothal. The fulfillment of this, my great desire, hath caused me to feel lenient toward mine enemies.”

“Have I not always asserted,” Diogenes broke in with comical solemnity—“always ass-asserted that your lordship was a noble and true gentleman?”

“Women, we know,” his lordship continued, ignoring the interruption, “are wont to be tenderhearted where their—their former swains are concerned. And I feel that if the jongejuffrouw herself did make appeal to me on your behalf, I would relent towards you.”

===Chinese===
他站在那里身子发晃，像是颇难保持平衡。一只手仍攀着桌沿，另一只手里拎着那满斟的酒壶。

「贝雷斯坦家的吉尔达小姐，」斯托滕贝格接着说，「今夜要与我共进晚餐，庆贺我们的订婚。这桩夙愿得偿，叫我对我的仇人们也宽大起来。」

「我不是一贯都断言么，」第欧根尼带着滑稽的庄严插进来说——「一贯断——断言，勋爵大人您是位高贵而真诚的绅士？」

「女人嘛，我们都知道，」大人只管往下说，全不理会这一岔，「凡牵涉到她们——她们旧日情郎的地方，心肠总是软的。我觉着，若是小姐她本人替你向我求情，我兴许就对你网开一面。」

===Original===
“B-b-but would that not be an awkward—a very awkward decision for your lordship?” Diogenes riposted, turning round vacant eyes on Stoutenburg.

“Awkward? How so?”

“If I do not hang, the jongejuffrouw, ’stead of being my widow, would still be my wife. And the laws of this country—”

“I have no concern with the laws of this country,” Stoutenburg rejoined drily, “in which, anyhow, you are an alien. As soon as the Archduchess our Liege Lady is once more mistress here, we shall again be at war with England.”

===Chinese===
「可——可——可是，这对勋爵大人您岂不是一桩尴尬——一桩十分尴尬的决断么？」第欧根尼回敬道，把一双茫然无神的眼睛转向斯托滕贝格。

「尴尬？此话怎讲？」

「我若不上绞架，小姐便不是我的寡妇，而依旧是我的妻子。而这个国家的律法——」

「这个国家的律法，与我何干，」斯托滕贝格干巴巴地应道，「何况你在这里本就是个外邦人。只等大公夫人（the Archduchess）——我们的宗主女主——重新做此地的主子，我们就要再度与英格兰开战。」

===Original===
“Poor England!”

Diogenes sighed, and solemnly wiped a tear from his blinking eyes.

“And every English *plepshurk* will be kicked out of the country. But that is neither here nor there.”

“Neither here nor there,” the other assented, with owlish gravity. “But before England is s-sh-s-swept off the map, my lordship, what will happen?”

===Chinese===
「可怜的英格兰！」

第欧根尼叹了口气，郑重其事地从眨个不停的眼睛上抹去一滴泪。

「到那时，每一个英格兰蠢货（plepshurk）都要被一脚踢出国境去。不过这都是题外话。」

「确是题外话，」对方一脸猫头鹰似的庄重表示赞同。「可是在英格兰被从地图上扫——扫——扫掉之前，我勋爵大人，还会先发生什么事呢？」

===Original===
“My marriage to the jongejuffrouw,” Stoutenburg replied curtly. “She hath consented to be my wife, and my wife she will be as soon as I have mind to take her. So you may drink to our union, sirrah. I’ll e’en pledge you in a cup.”

He poured himself out a goblet of wine, laughing to himself at his own ingenuity. That was the way to treat the *smeerlap*. Make him feel what a pitiable, abject knave he was! Then show him up before Gilda, just as he was—drunk, ragged, unkempt, an object of derision in his misfortune rather than of pity.

===Chinese===
「我与小姐的婚事，」斯托滕贝格简截地答道。「她既已应允做我的妻子，那么只等我想娶，她便是我的妻子。所以你不妨为我们的结合干一杯，小子。我也索性回敬你一盏。」

他给自己斟了一杯酒，暗自为自己的机智得意。对付这个龌龊货（smeerlap），就该用这个法子。先叫他自己掂量掂量，他是个多么可鄙、多么下贱的宵小！再把他带到吉尔达面前示众，就照他眼下这副模样——烂醉、褴褛、蓬头垢面，一桩落难之下惹人耻笑、而非惹人怜悯的活物。

===Original===
“Nay,” the rascal objected, his speech waxing thicker and his hand more unsteady, “I cannot pledge you, my lord, in drinking to your union with my own wife, unless—unless my friend Klaas will drink to that union, too. Mine own brother by the law, you see, my lord, and—”

“Mynheer Nicolaes will indeed drink to his sister’s happy union with me,” Stoutenburg retorted, with a sneer. “His presence here is a witness to my good intentions toward the wench. So you may drink, sirrah. The jongejuffrouw herself is overwilling to submit to my pleasure—”

But the imperious words were smothered in his throat, giving place to a fierce exclamation of choler. The blind man had at his invitation raised the jug of wine to his lips, but in the act his feet apparently slipped away from under him. The jug flew out of his hand, would have caught the Lord of Stoutenburg on the head had he not ducked just in time. But even so his Magnificence was hit on the shoulder by the heavy crystal vessel, and splashed from head to foot with the wine, whilst Diogenes collapsed on the floor with a shamed and bibulous laugh.

A string of savage oaths and tempestuous abuse poured from Stoutenburg’s lips, which were in truth livid with rage. Already Jan had rushed to his assistance, snatched up a serviette from the table, and soon contrived to wipe his lordship’s doublet clean.

===Chinese===
「那不行，」这无赖推拒道，舌头发僵，手也越发不稳，「我不能跟您干这一杯，勋爵大人，喝的是您跟我自己妻子的结合——除非——除非我的朋友克拉斯也来为这门结合干一杯。论律法，他可是我嫡亲的内兄呢，您明白么，勋爵大人，而且——」

「尼古拉斯老爷必定会为他妹妹与我的美满结合干杯，」斯托滕贝格狞笑着回道。「他人在这里，便是我对那丫头心怀善意的见证。所以你只管喝，小子。小姐她自己也千肯万肯，巴不得顺从我的心意——」

可这专横的话音在他喉咙里被生生闷住了，让位给一声狂怒的暴喝。盲人应着他方才那句相邀，已把那壶酒举到唇边；可就在举杯的当口，他的双脚显然从身子底下一滑。酒壶脱手飞出，若不是斯托滕贝格勋爵躲闪及时，正要磕在他脑袋上；饶是如此，这位大人阁下还是被那沉重的水晶器皿砸中了肩膀，酒浆从头到脚浇了他一身，而第欧根尼则带着一声羞惭的醉笑瘫倒在地板上。

一串凶蛮的咒骂和暴风雨般的斥责从斯托滕贝格唇间倾泻而出——那两片嘴唇实在已气得发青。扬早已抢上前去伺候，从桌上抓起一方餐巾，三下两下便把大人的紧身上衣擦拭干净了。

===Original===
The blind man in the meanwhile did his best to hoist himself up on his feet once more, clung to the edge of the table; but the sight of him released the last floodgate of Stoutenburg’s tempestuous wrath. He turned with a vicious snarl upon the unfortunate man, and it would indeed have fared ill with the defenceless creature, for the Lord of Stoutenburg was not wont to measure his blows by the helplessness of his victims, had not a sudden exclamation from Nicolaes stayed the hand that was raised to strike.

“Gilda!” the young man cried impulsively.

Stoutenburg’s arm dropped to his side. He turned toward the door. Gilda had just entered with her father, and was coming slowly down the room.

===Chinese===
这当口，盲人正竭力想再把自己从地上架起来，攀住桌沿；可他这副模样，偏偏冲开了斯托滕贝格狂怒的最后一道闸门。他狞恶地咆哮一声，朝那不幸的人转过身去——斯托滕贝格勋爵出手，向来不肯看受害者无助的分上减轻分毫——若不是尼古拉斯一声突兀的惊呼拉住了那只扬起来要打的手，这手无寸铁的可怜虫真要大祸临头了。

「吉尔达！」年轻人脱口喊道。

斯托滕贝格的手臂垂落到身侧。他朝门口转过身去。吉尔达刚随她父亲进门，正缓步向房里走来。

## XII Tears, Sighs, Hearts / XII 眼泪、叹息与心

### I / 一

===Original===
Gilda caught sight of her beloved the moment she entered. To say that their eyes met would indeed be folly. Certain it is, however, that the blind man turned his sightless gaze in her direction. She only gave a gasp, pressed her hands to her heart as if the pain there was unendurable, and at the moment even the beauty of her face was marred by the look of soul-racking misery in her eyes and the quivering lines around her mouth.

The next moment, even while Jan and the soldiers retired, closing the doors behind them, she was in her husband’s arms. Ay, even though Stoutenburg tried to intercept her. She did not hear his mocking laugh, or her brother’s vigorous protest, nor yet her father’s cry of horror. She just clung to him who, blind, fallen, degraded an you will, was still the beloved of her heart, the man to whom she had dedicated her soul.

She swallowed her tears, too proud to allow those who had wrought his ruin to see how mortally she was hurt.

===Chinese===
吉尔达（Gilda）一进门，便一眼望见了她的心上人。若说他们四目相对，那实在是句傻话。不过有一点确凿无疑：那盲人确曾把他那看不见的凝望，转向了她所在的方向。她只倒抽了一口气，双手紧紧按在心口，仿佛那里的疼痛已叫人无法忍受；那一刻，就连她脸上的美貌，也被眼中那副摧心剖肺的愁苦、嘴角颤抖的纹路损毁了。

转眼之间，就在扬（Jan）和士兵们退出去、随手掩上房门的当儿，她已扑进了丈夫的怀抱。是的，哪怕斯托滕贝格（Stoutenburg）想半路拦她。她没有听见他嘲弄的笑声，没有听见她哥哥激烈的反对，也没有听见她父亲惊骇的叫喊。她只是紧紧偎着他——瞎了也罢，倒了也罢，随你说他沦落成了什么也罢——他终究仍是她心头的爱人，是她把灵魂托付出去的那个男人。

她把眼泪咽了回去——她太高傲了，不肯让这些毁了他的人看见，她伤得有多重。

===Original===
She passed her delicate hands, fragrant as the petals of flowers, over his grimy face, those poor, stricken eyes, the noble brow so deeply furrowed with pain. She murmured words of endearment and of tenderness such as a mother might find to soothe the trouble of a suffering child. All in a moment. Stoutenburg had not even the time to interfere, to utter the savage oaths which rose from his vengeful heart at sight of the loving pity which this beautiful woman lavished on so contemptible an object.

Nor had the blind man time to encircle that exquisite form in his trembling arms. He had put them out at first, with a pathetic gesture of infinite longing. It was just a flash, a vision of his past self, an oblivion of the hideous, appalling present. Her arms at that moment were round his neck, her head against his breast, her soft, fair hair against his lips.

===Chinese===
她把那双纤手——花瓣般芬芳的纤手——抚过他污秽的脸，抚过那双可怜的、惨遭摧残的眼睛，抚过那因剧痛而刻下深深皱纹的高贵额头。她喃喃说着怜爱温存的话，就像母亲哄慰受苦的孩子时寻得的那类话语。而这一切只在一瞬间。斯托滕贝格甚至来不及插手，来不及骂出那些从复仇心里涌起的狠毒誓言——眼见这样一位美丽的女子，把满怀的爱怜如此挥霍在一个如此卑贱的物件身上。

那盲人也没来得及用颤抖的双臂环住那娇美的身躯。起初他确实伸出了双臂，一副无限渴慕的可怜姿态。那只是一霎——他旧日自我的一个投影，一时忘却了这可怖骇人的现在。那一刻，她的手臂环着他的脖颈，她的头贴着他的胸膛，她那柔软的金发拂着他的嘴唇。

### II / 二

===Original===
Then something happened. A magnetic current seemed to pass through the air. Diogenes freed himself with a sudden jerk from Gilda’s clinging arms, staggered back against the table, swaying on his feet and uttering an inane laugh; whilst she, left standing alone, turned wide, bewildered eyes on her brother Nicolaes, who happened to be close to her at the moment. I think that she was near to unconsciousness then, and that she would have fallen, but that the burgomaster stepped quickly to her side and put his arms round her.

“May God punish you,” he muttered between his teeth, and turned to Stoutenburg, who had watched the whole scene with a sinister scowl, “for this wanton and unnecessary cruelty!”

“You wrong me, mynheer,” Stoutenburg retorted, with a shrug. “I but tried to make your daughter’s decision easier for her.”

Then, as the burgomaster made no reply, but, with grim, set look on his face, drew his daughter gently down to the nearest chair, Stoutenburg went on lightly, speaking directly to Gilda:

===Chinese===
接着，出了一桩事。仿佛有一道磁流凌空掠过。第欧根尼（Diogenes）猛地一挣，从吉尔达紧搂的双臂中脱出身来，踉跄着退到桌边，脚下晃晃悠悠，嘴里发出一阵痴傻的笑；而她被撇下独自立着，睁大一双惶惑的眼睛，望向此刻恰在近旁的哥哥尼古拉斯（Nicolaes）。我想她那时已濒于昏厥，若不是市长（burgomaster）抢步到她身边、伸臂将她扶住，她早已倒在地上了。

「愿上帝惩罚你，」他从齿缝里挤出这句话，一面转向斯托滕贝格——后者自始至终蹙着一副阴沉的怒容，冷眼旁观这一幕——「为这任意妄为、全无必要的残忍！」

「您冤枉我了，老爷，」斯托滕贝格耸耸肩回敬道。「我不过是想让令嫒的决定来得容易些。」

市长没有作声，只板着一张铁青的脸，把女儿轻轻搀到近旁的椅子上坐下；斯托滕贝格便又轻飘飘地接了下去，这回径直冲着吉尔达说：

===Original===
“In the course of my travels, mejuffrouw, I came across a wise philosopher in Italy. He was a man whom an adverse fate had robbed of most things that he held precious; but he told me that he had quite succeeded in conquering adversity by the following means. He would gaze dispassionately on the objects of his past desires, see their defects, appraise them at their just value, and in every case he found that their loss was not so irreparable as he had originally believed.”

“A fine moral lesson, my lord,” the burgomaster interposed, seeing that Gilda either would not or could not speak as yet. “But I do not see its point.”

“ ’Tis a simple one, mynheer,” Stoutenburg retorted coldly. “I pray you, look on the man to whom, an you had your way, you would even now link your daughter.”

Instinctively Beresteyn turned his lowering gaze in the direction to which his lordship now pointed with a persuasive gesture. Diogenes was standing beside the table, his powerful frame drawn up to its full height, his sightless eyes blinking and gleaming with weird inconsequence in the flickering light of the candles. His hands were clasped behind his back, and on his face there was a curious expression which the burgomaster was not shrewd enough to define﻿—one of self-deprecation, yet withal of introspection and of detachment, as if the helpless body alone were present and the mind had gone a-roaming in the land of dreams. The burgomaster tried manfully to conceal the look of half-contemptuous pity which, much against his will, had crept into his eyes.

===Chinese===
「在我游历的途中，小姐（mejuffrouw），我在意大利遇到过一位睿智的哲人。他是个被厄运夺去了大半珍爱之物的人；可他告诉我，他用下面这个法子，相当成功地降服了厄运。他会冷眼凝视自己往日欲求的对象，看清它们的缺陷，给它们估一个恰如其分的身价；而每一次他都发现，失去它们并不像他起初所以为的那样无可挽回。」

「好一堂精深的道德功课，勋爵大人，」市长插话道，他看出吉尔达一时既不肯、也不能开口。「只是我看不出它有什么要点。」

「要点简单得很，老爷，」斯托滕贝格冷冷地回敬。「我请您瞧瞧这个人——若依了您的心意，您此刻就要把令嫒许配给他的这个人。」

贝雷斯坦（Beresteyn）身不由己，把他阴沉的目光转向勋爵此刻打着手势殷殷指点的方向。第欧根尼正立在桌旁，魁伟的身躯挺得笔直，失明的双眼在摇曳的烛光里眨动，闪出一种怪诞而散乱的光。他两手交叠在背后，脸上带着一种奇特的表情——市长还不够精明，说不出那究竟是什么——那是一种自嘲，却又兼有内省与超然，仿佛只余这具无助的躯壳留在此地，心灵早已漫游到梦的国度里去了。市长竭力想掩住眼中那股不由自主渗出来的、半含轻蔑的怜悯。

===Original===
“The man,” he rejoined calmly, “is what Fate and a dastard’s hand have made him, my lord. Many a fine work of God hath been marred by an evildoer’s action.”

“That is as may be mynheer,” Stoutenburg riposted coolly. “But ’tis of the present and of the future you have to think now﻿—not of the past.”

“Even so, my lord, I would sooner see my daughter in the arms of the stricken lion than in those of a wily jackal.”

“Am I the wily jackal?” Stoutenburg put in, with a sneer. Then, as the burgomaster made no reply, he added tersely: “I see that the jongejuffrouw hath told you﻿—”

“Everything,” Beresteyn assented calmly.

===Chinese===
「这个人，」他平静地答道，「是命运加一个懦夫的手把他作践成这般模样的，勋爵大人。上帝的许多精美造物，都曾被恶人的行径毁坏过。」

「也许是这样，老爷，」斯托滕贝格冷冷地反唇相讥。「可您如今该考虑的是现在和将来——不是过去。」

「即便如此，勋爵大人，我也宁肯看我女儿投入那头受创的狮子的怀抱，也不愿看她落入一只奸猾的豺狼的怀抱。」

「我就是那只奸猾的豺狼么？」斯托滕贝格冷笑着插嘴。见市长不作声，他又简短地补了一句：「看来小姐（jongejuffrouw）已经把一切都告诉您了——」

「一切都告诉了。」贝雷斯坦平静地应道。

===Original===
“And that I await your blessing on our union?”

“My blessing you cannot have, my lord, as you well know,” the burgomaster retorted firmly. “ ’Twas blasphemy to invoke the name of God on such an unholy alliance. My daughter is the lawfully wedded wife of an English gentleman, Sir Percy Blakeney by name, and until the law of this country doth sever those bonds she cannot wed another.”

Stoutenburg gave a strident laugh.

“That is, indeed, unfortunate for the English gentleman with the high-sounding name,” he said, with a sneer, “whom I gravely suspect of being naught but the common varlet whom we all know so well in Haarlem. But, gentleman or churl,” he added, with a cynical shrug, “ ’tis all one to me. He hangs tomorrow, unless﻿—”

===Chinese===
「也告诉您，我正等着您为我们的事赐福了？」

「我的福您是得不到的，勋爵大人，这一点您清楚得很，」市长斩钉截铁地回敬。「在这等邪恶的结合上祈求上帝的名号，本身就是渎神。我的女儿是一位英格兰绅士明媒正娶的妻子——此人名叫珀西·布莱克尼爵士（Sir Percy Blakeney）——只要这个国家的法律不曾解除这层纽带，她就不能再嫁旁人。」

斯托滕贝格发出一声刺耳的大笑。

「这对那位名号响亮的英格兰绅士来说，的确是件不幸的事，」他讥诮地说，「我可深深疑心，此人无非就是咱们在哈勒姆（Haarlem）都熟得不能再熟的那个下贱恶棍。不过，绅士也罢，粗汉也罢，」他玩世不恭地耸了耸肩，又补上一句，「在我这儿全是一回事。他明天就上绞架，除非——」

===Original===
A loud cry of burning indignation escaped the burgomaster’s lips.

“You would not further provoke the wrath of God,” he exclaimed, “by this foul and cowardly crime!”

“And why not, I pray you?” the other coolly retorted. “Nor do I think that the Almighty would greatly care what happened to this drunken knave. The refuse of human kind, the halt, the lame, and the blind, are best out of the way.”

“A man, my lord,” the burgomaster protested, “who, when he had you in his power, generously spared your life!”

“The more fool he!” Stoutenburg riposted drily. “ ’Tis my turn now. He hangs tomorrow, unless, indeed﻿—”

“Unless, what, my lord?”

===Chinese===
一声怒火灼人的高喊，从市长口中迸了出来。

「您难道还要以这桩肮脏而怯懦的罪行，」他喊道，「去进一步惹动上帝的义怒么！」

「倒要请问，为什么不呢？」对方冷冷地回敬。「我也不见得认为，全能的上帝会多么在意这个醉鬼无赖的死活。人类中的渣滓——瘸的、跛的、瞎的——还是及早让路的好。」

「这个人，勋爵大人，」市长抗议道，「当年他大权在握、把您攥在掌心里的时候，可是慷慨地饶过您一条性命的！」

「那他就更是个傻瓜！」斯托滕贝格干巴巴地反击。「如今轮到我了。他明天就上绞架，除非当真——」

「除非什么，勋爵大人？」

===Original===
“Unless,” Stoutenburg went on, with an evil leer, “my future wife will deign to plead with me for him﻿—with a kiss.”

A groan like that of a wounded beast broke from the burgomaster’s heavy heart. For a moment a light that was almost murderous gleamed in his eyes. His fists were clenched; he murmured a dark threat against the man who goaded him well-nigh to madness. Then, suddenly, he met Stoutenburg’s mocking glance fixed upon him, and a huge sob rose in his throat, almost choking him. Gilda, with a pitiful moan, had hidden her face against her father’s sleeve.

“ ’Tis but anticipating the happy time by a few hours,” Stoutenburg went on, with calm cynicism. “But I have a fancy to hold my future wife in my arms now﻿—at this moment﻿—and to grant her in exchange for her first willing kiss the life of a miserable wretch whose life or death are, in truth, of no account to me.”

He took a step or two forward in the direction where Gilda sat, clinging with desperate misery to her father. Then, as the burgomaster, superb with indignation, grand in his dignity, instinctively interposed his burly figure between his daughter and the man whom she loathed, Stoutenburg added, with well-assumed carelessness:

“If the jongejuffrouw prefers to put off the happy moment until we are alone in my camp tomorrow, we’ll say no more about it. Let the rogue hang; I care not!”

===Chinese===
「除非，」斯托滕贝格接下去说，眼里闪着一道恶毒的邪光，「我未来的妻子肯赏脸替他向我求情——用一个吻。」

一声好似受伤野兽的哀号，从市长沉重的心底迸了出来。有一瞬间，他眼里几乎闪出一道杀气。他的双拳紧握；他低声咕哝出一句阴沉的威胁，咒那个把他撩拨得几近发狂的人。可突然间，他撞上了斯托滕贝格钉在他身上那嘲弄的目光，一团巨大的呜咽涌上喉头，几乎把他噎住。吉尔达发出一声凄楚的哀鸣，把脸埋进了父亲的衣袖。

「这不过是把良辰提前几个钟头罢了，」斯托滕贝格带着安之若素的玩世不恭接下去说。「可我偏偏起了兴致，想现在——就在这一刻——把我未来的妻子搂进怀里，用她头一个心甘情愿的吻，换给她一条可怜虫的性命——说实话，这条命是死是活，于我都无关紧要。」

他朝吉尔达坐着的方向迈出一两步——她正万般凄苦地紧偎着父亲。市长义愤填膺，威严凛然，本能地把魁伟的身躯横在女儿与她所憎恶的那个男人之间；斯托滕贝格便装出一副满不在乎的神气，添了一句：

「若是小姐宁愿把这良辰推迟到明天，等我俩单独在我的营帐里，那咱们就不必多说了。让那无赖上绞架好了；我才不在乎！」

===Original===
“My lord,”﻿—the burgomaster spoke once more in a vigorous protest, which, alas, he knew to be futile﻿—“what you suggest is monstrous, inhuman! God will never permit﻿—”

“I pray you, mynheer,” Stoutenburg broke in fiercely, “let us leave the Almighty out of our affairs. I have read my Bible as assiduously as you when I was younger, and in it I learned that God hath enjoined all wives to submit themselves to their husbands. A kiss from my betrothed, a word or gentle pleading, are little enough to ask in exchange for an act of clemency. And you, Heer Burgomaster, do but stiffen my will by your interference. Will you, at least, let the jongejuffrouw decide on the matter for herself, and, in her interests and your own, give to all that she does your unqualified consent!”

“My consent you’ll never wring from me, as you well know, my lord. I and my daughter are powerless to withstand your might, but if we bend to the yoke it is because it hath pleased God that we should wear it, not because we submit with a free will. By exulting in such a monstrous crime you do but add to the loathing which we both feel for you﻿—”

“Silence!” Stoutenburg broke in fiercely. “Silence, you dolt! What good, think you, you do yourself or your daughter by provoking me beyond endurance? She knows my decision, and so, methinks, do you. If the jongejuffrouw feels such unqualified hatred for me, let her return to your protecting arms and leave Amersfoort to its fate. As for that sightless varlet, let him hang, I say! I am a fool, indeed to listen to your gibberish! Jan!” he called, and strode to the door with a great show of determination, staking his all now on this card which he had decided to play.

===Chinese===
「勋爵大人，」——市长又一次激昂地抗议道，唉，他自己也明白这抗议是徒劳的——「您所提议的事丧尽天良，毫无人性！上帝绝不会容许——」

「我求您了，老爷，」斯托滕贝格粗暴地打断他，「咱们的事，别把全能的上帝扯进来。我年轻的时候读《圣经》，用功不亚于您，从里头我学到：上帝吩咐过，一切做妻子的都当顺服自己的丈夫。从我未婚妻那里讨一个吻、一句温言或一声软语恳求，来换一桩宽恕之恩，这实在算不得什么苛求。而您，市长大人（Heer Burgomaster），横加干涉只会叫我的意志更硬。您至少肯不肯让小姐自己来拿这个主意，并且——为她着想，也为您自己着想——对她所做的一切，给一声毫无保留的允准！」

「我的允准，您永远也别想从我这里逼出来，这一点您清楚得很，勋爵大人。我同我女儿都无力抗拒您的强权；可我们若俯首就轭，那是因为上帝乐意叫我们戴上它，绝不是因为我们心甘情愿地降服。您为这样一桩滔天罪行扬扬得意，只会叫我父女俩对您的憎恶更添一层——」

「住口！」斯托滕贝格狂暴地打断他。「住口，你这蠢材！你以为把我撩拨到忍无可忍，对你自己或你女儿有什么好处？她知道我的决定，我想您也知道。小姐若当真对我怀着这般不共戴天的憎恨，那就让她回到您的庇护里去，听凭阿默斯福特（Amersfoort）听天由命好了。至于那个瞎眼的恶棍，我说了，让他上绞架去！我真是天下第一大傻瓜，居然听你咿哩哇啦！扬！」他喊了一声，摆出一副斩钉截铁的架势，大步朝门口走去——此刻他把全部身家都押在了他决定打出的这张牌上。

===Original===
But the card was a winning one, as well he knew. Already Gilda, as if moved by an unseen voice, had jumped to her feet and intercepted him ere he reached the door. Her whole appearance had changed﻿—the expression of her eyes, her tone, her gestures.

“My father is overwrought, my lord,” she said firmly. “He hath already promised me that he would offer no opposition to my wishes.”

She looked him straight in the eyes, and he returned her gaze, his restless eyes seeming to search her very soul. She had, in truth, changed most markedly. She was, of course, afraid﻿—afraid for that miserable *plepshurk’s* life. But the change was something more than that﻿—at least, Stoutenburg chose to think so. There was something in her glance at this moment that he did not quite understand, that he did not dare understand. A wavering﻿—almost he would have called it a softness, had he dared. He came nearer to her, and, though at first she drew back from him, she presently held her ground, still gazing on him like a bird when it is fascinated and cannot move.

Now he was quite sure that her blue eyes looked less hard, and certainly her mouth was less tightly set. Her lips were slightly parted, and her breath came quick and panting. Ah, women were queer creatures! Had Nicolaes been right when he quoted the English play? Gilda had certainly begun by falling against that contemptible rascal’s breast, but since then? Had her wayward fancy been repelled by that whole air of physical degradation which emanated from the once brilliant cavalier, or had it been merely dazzled by visions of power and of wealth, which had their embodiment in him who was her future lord?

===Chinese===
可这张牌是张赢牌，他心里再清楚不过。吉尔达已像被一个看不见的声音驱使着，霍然跳起身，抢在他走到门口之前拦住了他。她整个人的模样都变了——眼神、语调、手势，全变了。

「我父亲是太激动了，勋爵大人，」她坚决地说。「他已经答应过我，绝不对我的心愿横加阻挠。」

她直直地望进他的眼睛，他也回望着她，那双不安分的眼睛仿佛要一直搜进她的灵魂。她确确实实变了，变得再明显不过。她自然是害怕——为那个可怜的蠢货（*plepshurk*）的性命害怕。可这变化还不止于此——至少，斯托滕贝格愿意这么想。她此刻的目光里有种他捉摸不透的东西，一种他不敢去捉摸的东西。一丝动摇——若他敢承认，他几乎要称之为一种柔情了。他向她走近；起初她从他面前缩了缩，随即便站定了脚跟，仍旧凝望着他，像一只被迷住、动弹不得的鸟。

这下他确信无疑了：她那双蓝眼睛不那么冷硬了，她的嘴也确实不再抿得那么紧了。她的双唇微微张开，呼吸急促，带着喘息。啊，女人真是些奇怪的造物！尼古拉斯引那句英国戏文的时候，莫非真说对了？吉尔达起初确是扑在了那个可鄙恶棍的怀里，可打那以后呢？她那任性的情意，是被昔日那位煊赫的骑士周身散发出的那副落魄潦倒的气象吓退了呢，还是单单被权势与财富的种种幻象晃花了眼——而这些幻象，恰恰化身为她未来的夫君？

===Original===
He himself could not say. All that he knew, all that he felt of a certainty now, was that he held more than one winning card in this gamble for possession of an exquisite and desirable woman. Still holding her gaze, he took her hands. She did not resist, did not attempt to draw away from him, and he murmured softly:

“What are your wishes, *myn engel*?”

“To submit to your will, my lord,” she replied firmly.

“At last!” he exclaimed, on a note of triumph, drew her still closer to him. “A kiss, fair one, to clinch this bargain, which hath made me the happiest of men!”

===Chinese===
他自己也说不上来。此刻他所知道、所确凿感到的只有一件：在这场争夺一个娇美可人之女子的赌局里，他手里握着的不止一张赢牌。他依旧望着她的眼睛，握住了她的双手。她没有抗拒，没有试图从他手里抽身，于是他柔声喃喃道：

「您的心愿是什么呢，我的天使（*myn engel*）？」

「顺从您的意志，勋爵大人，」她坚决地答道。

「总算盼到了！」他扬着得胜的声调喊道，把她搂得更紧。「一个吻，美人儿，好敲定这桩交易——它已叫我成了天下最幸福的男人！」

===Original===
He had lost his head for the moment. Satisfaction, and an almost feverish sense of exultation, had turned his blood to liquid fire. All that he saw was this lovely woman, whom he had nearly conquered. Nearly, but not quite. At his desire for a kiss he felt that she stiffened. She closed her eyes, and even her lips became bloodless. She appeared on the verge of a swoon. Bah! Even this phase would pass away. Nicolaes was right. Women reserved their contempt for weaklings. In the end ’twas the master whom they adored.

“A kiss, fair one!” he called again. “And the rogue shall live or hang according as your lips are sweet or bitter!”

He was on the point of snatching that kiss at last, when suddenly there came so violent a crash that the whole room shook with the concussion, and even the windows rattled in their frames. The blind man, more unsteady than ever on his feet, had tried to get hold of a chair, lost his balance in the act, and, in the endeavour to save himself from falling, had lurched so clumsily against the table that it overturned, and all the objects upon it﻿—silver, crystal, china dishes, and candelabra﻿—fell with a deafening clatter on the floor.

Stoutenburg, uttering one of his favourite oaths, had instinctively turned to see whence had come this terrific noise. In turning, his hold on Gilda’s wrists had slightly relaxed; sufficiently, at any rate, to enable her to free herself from his grasp and to seek shelter once more beside her father. Diogenes alone had remained unruffled through the commotion. Indeed, he appeared wholly unconscious that he had brought it about. He had collapsed amidst the litter, and now sprawled on the floor, surrounded by a medley of broken glass, guttering candles, hot food and liquor, convulsed with laughter, whist his huge, dark eyes, with the dilated pupils and pale, narrow circles of blue light, looked strangely ghostlike in the gloom.

“Who in thunder,” he muttered inarticulately, “is making this confounded din?”

===Chinese===
他一时忘乎所以了。满足，加上一阵近乎狂热的欢腾，把他的血化成了流火。他眼里只剩下这个可爱的女人——这个他差不多已征服到手的女人。差不多，但还差一点。索吻的欲念一起，他觉出她僵住了。她闭上了眼睛，连嘴唇都失了血色。她眼看就要晕厥。呸！这个阶段也会过去的。尼古拉斯说得对。女人的轻蔑是留给弱者的。到头来，她们崇拜的终是主人。

「一个吻，美人儿！」他又喊道。「那无赖是活是吊，就全看您的嘴唇是甜是苦了！」

他眼看终于要把那个吻抢到手了，突然一声轰响，猛烈得整间屋子都随之震颤，连窗框里的玻璃都格格作响。那盲人本就站得比先前更不稳当，这时想抓一把椅子，手到之处却失了平衡；为了不让自己摔倒，他又笨拙地朝桌子猛撞过去，竟把桌子撞翻了，桌上的所有物件——银器、水晶、瓷盘，还有烛台——噼里啪啦一阵震耳欲聋，全都砸落在地板上。

斯托滕贝格骂出一句他心爱的口头咒骂，本能地转过身去看这骇人的声响从何而来。一转身间，他攥着吉尔达双腕的手劲略略一松；反正松得足够她从他掌中挣脱出来，重新躲回父亲身边去了。这场骚动里，唯有第欧根尼一人泰然自若。真的，他看上去浑然不觉这场乱子是自己惹出来的。他瘫倒在这一片狼藉中间，此刻四仰八叉地摊在地板上，四周散乱着碎玻璃、淌蜡的蜡烛、热腾腾的酒菜；他笑得浑身乱颤，而他那双又大又黑的眼睛——瞳孔放大，罩着一圈窄窄的、苍白的蓝光——在幽暗里显得鬼气森森。

「到底是什么人，」他口齿不清地咕哝着，「在弄出这该死的喧响？」

### III / 三

===Original===
At the noise, too, the men had come running in from the hall. The sound had been akin to the detonation of a dozen pistols, and they had rushed along, prepared for a fight. With the fall of the candelabra, the vast banqueting hall had suddenly been plunged into semidarkness. Only a couple of wax candles in tall sconces, which had originally set on the sideboard, vaguely illumined the disorderly scene.

Diogenes, with his infectious laugh, did in truth succeed in warding off the punishment which his Magnificence already held in preparation for him. As it was, Stoutenburg caught sight of Gilda’s look of anxiety, and this at once put him into a rare good humour. He had had his wish. Gilda had been almost kind, had practically yielded to him in the presence of the man whom he desired to humiliate and to wound, as he himself had been humiliated and wounded in the past.

Whether the blind man’s keen sense of hearing had taken in every detail of the scene, it was of course impossible to say. But one thing he must have heard﻿—the brief soliloquy at the door, when Gilda, in response to his ardent query. “What are your wishes, *myn engel*?” had replied quite firmly: “To submit to your will, my lord!” That moment must, in truth, have been more galling and more bitter to the once gallant Laughing Cavalier than the rattle of the rope upon the gallows, or the first consciousness that he was irremediably blind.

Indeed, Stoutenburg had had something more than his wish. To make a martyr of the rogue, he would have told you, was not part of his desire. All that he wanted was to obliterated the man’s former brilliant personality from Gilda’s mind; that he should henceforth dwell in her memory as she last saw him, abject in his obvious impotence, owing his life to the woman whom he had wooed and conquered in the past with the high hand of a reckless adventurer. After that, the rogue might hang or perish in a ditch: his lordship did not care. What happened to blind men in these days of fighting when none but the best men had a chance to live at all, he had never troubled his head to inquire. At any rate, he knew that a sightless lion was less harmful than a keen-eyed mouse. Ah, in truth he had had more than his wish and satisfied now as to the present and the future, the thought that the moment had come to let well alone, and to remove from Gilda’s sight the spectacle which, by some subtle reaction, might turn her heart back to pity for the knave. He gave Jan a significant nod.

===Chinese===
听到这声响，士兵们也从门厅那边奔了进来。那声音好比十几支手枪一齐炸响，他们是冲进来的，个个准备厮杀一场。烛台倒地之后，宽敞的宴会厅霎时陷入半明半暗。只有原先设在餐具柜上的那对高脚烛架里的两支蜡烛，还隐隐照亮着这片凌乱的景象。

第欧根尼凭着他那富有感染力的大笑，当真躲过了大人阁下早已为他备下的那顿惩罚。事已如此：斯托滕贝格瞥见了吉尔达眼里的焦灼，而这顿时叫他生出一副难得的好兴致。他如愿以偿了。吉尔达几乎是温存的，几乎当着那个他想羞辱、想刺伤的男人的面向他屈服了——一如他自己往昔所受的羞辱与创伤。

那盲人敏锐的听觉是否把这一幕的每个细节都收进了耳中，自然无从断言。但有一件事他必定听到了——门边那段简短的对答：斯托滕贝格热切地问「您的心愿是什么呢，我的天使（*myn engel*）？」而吉尔达斩钉截铁地答了「顺从您的意志，勋爵大人！」那一刻，对当年那位豪迈的笑面骑士（Laughing Cavalier）而言，想必比绞架上绳索的咔啦声响、比头一回意识到自己已无可救药地失明，更加锥心，更加苦涩。

的确，斯托滕贝格所得的已超出了他的心愿。他会告诉您：把这无赖捧成烈士，并不在他的心愿之内。他要的，只是把这个男人昔日煊赫的人格从吉尔达心里抹去；让他在她的记忆里，从此只留下她最后所见的那副模样：一望而知的穷途无力，一条性命还是仰仗那个女人讨来的——而当年，他曾以亡命冒险家的铁腕向她求过爱、把她征服过。在那之后，这无赖是上绞架还是死在阴沟里：勋爵大人一概不放在心上。至于在这人人杀伐的年月里——那时节，除了顶顶尖的人物，谁都难有活路——盲人们会落个什么下场，他从不曾费神过问。无论如何，他知道一头失明的狮子，总比一只目光锐利的老鼠少些危害。啊，说实在的，他所得的已超过了心愿；如今对现在与将来都放了心，他便想到：见好就收的时候到了，该把这场面从吉尔达眼前撤下去了——那副景象，说不定会借着什么微妙的反应，把她的心重新拉回到对那无赖的怜悯上去。他朝扬意味深长地点了点头。

===Original===
But Gilda, whose glowing eyes had watched his every movement, was quick to interpose.

“My lord,” she cried in protest, “I hold you to your bargain!”

“Have no fear, *myn chat*,” he answered suavely. “I will not repudiate it. The fellow’s life is safe enough whilst you and the Heer Burgomaster honour me by supping with me. After that, the decision rests with you. As I said just now, he shall live or hang according as your lips are sweet or bitter. For the nonce, I am wearied and hungry. We’ll sup first, so please you.”

===Chinese===
可吉尔达那双灼灼的眼睛一直盯着他的一举一动，立刻抢上来拦阻。

「勋爵大人，」她抗声道，「说定的交易，我是要您践诺的！」

「不必担心，我的宝贝（*myn chat*），」他温文地答道。「我不会赖账的。只要您和市长大人赏脸，陪我共进这顿晚饭，这家伙的性命就稳妥得很。在那之后，决定权全在您手里。正像我方才说的，他是活是吊，全看您的嘴唇是甜是苦。眼下呢，我又累又饿。若蒙俯允，咱们先用晚饭。」

===Original===
And Gilda had to stand by whilst she saw her husband dragged away from her presence. He offered no resistance; indeed, accepted the situation with that good-humoured philosophy which was so characteristic of him. But, oh, if she could have conveyed to him by a look all the tenderness, the sorrow, the despair, that was torturing her heart! If she could have run to him just once more, to whisper into his ear those burning words of love which would have eased his pain and hers!

If she could have defied that abominable tyrant who gloated over her misery, and, hand in hand with her beloved, have met death by his side, with his arms around her, her spirit wedded to his, ere they appeared together before the judgement seat of God!

But, as that arrogant despot had reminded her, she had even lost the freedom to die. The destinies of her native city were in her hands. Unless she bowed her willing neck to his will, Amersfoort and all its citizens would be wiped off the face of the earth. And as she watched the chosen of her heart led like a captive lion to humiliation if not to death those monstrous words rang in her ears, that surely must provoke the wrath of God.

Therefore, she watched his departure dry-eyed and motionless. Ay! envying him in her heart, that he, at least, was not called upon to make such an appalling sacrifice as lay now before her. She had indeed come to that sublimity of human suffering that she almost wished to see those dear, sightless eyes closed in their last long sleep, rather than that he should be forced to endure what to him would be ten thousand times worse than death﻿—her submission to that miscreant﻿—her willing union; and he, ignorant of how the tyrant had wrung this submission from her.

===Chinese===
吉尔达只得眼睁睁站在一旁，看着丈夫被人从她面前拖走。他没有反抗；真的，他以那种好性儿的达观接受了这个处境——那正是他独一份的脾气。可是，唉，她若能只用一个眼神，就把煎熬着她心的全部柔情、哀恸与绝望都传给他，该有多好！她若能再跑到他身边去一次，往他耳边轻轻送上那些滚烫的情话——那些本可以减轻他的痛、也减轻她的痛的情话，该有多好！

她若能反抗那个把她苦难当享受的可憎暴君，与她心爱的人手牵着手，在他身侧共赴一死——他搂着她，她的灵魂与他的结为一体，然后两人一同站到上帝的审判座前，该有多好！

可是，正如那个骄横的暴君提醒过她的，她连死的自由都已失掉。她故土城市的命运就攥在她手里。除非她心甘情愿地俯颈就范于他的意志，否则阿默斯福特和全城百姓就要从地面上被抹去。而当她望着自己心尖上选中的人，像一头被俘的狮子般被牵去受辱——若不是牵去赴死——那些字字句句便在她耳边轰响：那些必定会惹动上帝义怒的、伤天害理的话。

因此，她眼睛干干地、一动不动地目送他离去。是的！她心里在羡慕他——至少，没有人要他作出此刻横在她面前的这等骇人的牺牲。她实在已被逼到人类苦难的那种极致，几乎情愿看到那双亲爱的、失明的眼睛阖上，坠入最后的长眠，也不愿他被迫忍受那对他来说比死还难上一万倍的事——她向那个恶棍的屈从——她甘愿的结合；而他，对这暴君是如何从她身上逼出这份屈从的，一无所知。

## XIII The Stygian Creek / XIII 冥河之溪

### I / 一

===Original===
The Lord of Stoutenburg was conscious of a great feeling of relief when the blind man was finally removed from his presence. While the latter stood there, even in the abjectness of his plight, Stoutenburg felt that he was a living menace to the success of all his well-thought-out schemes. He kept his eyes fixed on Gilda with a warning look, that should be a reminder to her of the immutability of his resolve. He tried, in a manner, to surround her with a compelling fluid that would engulf her resistance and leave her weak and passive to his will.

There was of necessity a vast amount of confusion and din ere order was restored among the debris; and conversation was impossible in the midst of the clatter that was going on—men coming and going, the rattle of silver and glass. Gilda, the while, sat quite still, her blue eyes fixed with strange intensity on the door through which her beloved had disappeared. Her father stood beside her, holding her hand, and she rested her cheek against his.

The burgomaster, throughout the last scene, had not once looked at Diogenes. A dark, puzzled frown lingered between his brows whilst he stared moodily into the fire. He absolutely ignored the presence of his son, putting into practice his stern dictum that henceforth he had no son, whilst Nicolaes, who was becoming inured to his shameful position, put on a careless and jaunty air, spoke with easy familiarity to Stoutenburg, and peremptorily to the men.

Then at last the table was once more set, the candles relit, and the board again spread for supper. Stoutenburg, with an elegant flourish, invited his guests to sit, offered his arm to Gilda to lead her to the table. She, moved by a pathetic desire to conciliate him, a forlorn hope that a great show of submission on her part would soften his cruel heart and lighten the fate of her beloved, placed her hand upon his sleeve, and when she met his admiring glance a slight flush drove the pallor from her cheeks.

===Chinese===
那盲人终于被从眼前带走时，斯托滕贝格勋爵（The Lord of Stoutenburg）自觉大大松了一口气。方才那人站在那儿，纵然落魄到了那步田地，斯托滕贝格仍觉得他活着这件事本身，就是对他一切深思远虑的计划的一种活生生的威胁。他把目光钉在吉尔达（Gilda）身上，带着警示的神色，提醒她他的决心不可移易。他仿佛是在设法用一种逼人的磁流把她整个包围起来，要吞没她的抗拒，叫她软弱、驯从，任凭他的意志摆布。

瓦砾狼藉之间要恢复秩序，自然免不了一阵大乱与喧闹；人来人往，银器杯盘叮当作响，在这一片嘈杂里谁也没法交谈。吉尔达则始终静坐不动，一双蓝眼睛带着异样的专注，直望着她心上人消失的那扇门。父亲站在她身旁，握着她的手，她把脸颊偎在他的脸颊上。

在方才那一幕里，市长（burgomaster）一眼也不曾看第欧根尼（Diogenes）。他闷闷地盯着炉火，眉宇间凝着一道阴沉、困惑的皱痕。他全然无视儿子的存在，把那句严酷的断语付诸实行——从今往后，他没有这个儿子；而尼古拉斯（Nicolaes）呢，对自己可耻的处境已渐渐处之泰然，摆出一副满不在乎的轻狂神气，同斯托滕贝格熟不拘礼地谈笑，对手下的兵卒则颐指气使。

最后，餐桌终于重新摆好，蜡烛重新点上，晚餐又一次铺陈开来。斯托滕贝格风度翩翩地一躬身，请客人入席，又伸出手臂，要引吉尔达入座。她心里怀着一种可怜的、想与他修好的愿望，抱着一线渺茫的希望——但愿自己做出十足顺从的姿态，能软化他那颗狠心，减轻她心上人的厄运——便把手搭上他的衣袖；迎上他倾慕的目光时，一抹淡淡的红晕驱走了她颊上的苍白。

===Original===
“You are adorable, *myn geloof*!” he murmured.

He appeared highly elated, sat at the head of the table, with Gilda on his right and the burgomaster on his left, whilst Nicolaes sat beside his sister.

The two old crones served the supper, coming and going with a noiselessness and precision acquired in long service in the well-conducted house of the burgomaster. They knew the use of the two pronged silver utensils which Mynheer Beresteyn had acquired of late direct from France, where they were used at the table of gentlemen of quality for conveying food to the mouth. They knew how to remove each service from the centre of the table without unduly disturbing the guests, and how to replace one cloth with another the moment it became soiled with sauce or wine.

===Chinese===
「你真可爱，我的信仰（*myn geloof*）！」他喃喃道。

他显得兴高采烈，在桌首落座，吉尔达坐他右手，市长坐他左手，尼古拉斯则挨着妹妹坐下。

两个老丑婆子上菜伺候，来来去去，悄无声息、有条不紊——这是她们在市长治家有方的宅子里当差多年练出来的功夫。那两齿的银制餐具，贝雷斯坦老爷（Mynheer Beresteyn）新近才直接从法兰西购得——在那边，上流绅士的餐桌上就用它把食物送进口中——她们也懂得用法。她们知道怎样把一道道菜从桌子中央撤下而不致过分惊扰客人，也知道桌布一沾了酱汁或酒渍，就立刻换上新的。

===Original===
Jan stood at the Lord of Stoutenburg’s elbow and served him personally and with his own hands. Every dish, before it was handed to his lordship, was placed in front of the burgomaster, who was curtly bidden to taste of it. His Magnificence, adept in the poisoner’s art, was taking no risks himself.

The cook had done his best, and the supper was, I believe, excellent. The Oille, the most succulent of dishes, made up of quails, capons, and ducks and other tasty meats, was a marvel of gastronomic art, and so were the tureens of beef with cucumber and the breast of veal larded and garnished with hard-boiled eggs. In truth it was all a terrible waste, and sad to see such excellent fare laid before guests who hardly would touch a morsel. Gilda could not eat, her throat seemed to close up every time she tried to swallow. Indeed, she had to appeal to the very last shred of her pride to keep up a semblance of dignity before her enemy. The burgomaster, too, flushed with shame at the indignity put upon him, did no more than taste of the dishes as they were put before him by the surly Jan.

The Lord of Stoutenburg, on the other hand, put up a great show of hilarity, talked much and drank deeply, discussed in a loud, arrogant voice with Nicolaes the Archduchess’s plans for the subduing of the Netherlands. And Nicolaes, after he had imbibed two or three bumpers of heady Spanish wine, felt more assured, returned Gilda’s reproachful glances with indifference, and his father’s contempt with defiance.

===Chinese===
扬（Jan）侍立在斯托滕贝格勋爵肘边，亲自伸手为他布菜。每一道菜，在呈到他大人面前之前，都先摆到市长面前，再简慢地吩咐市长先尝一口。这位大人阁下深谙下毒的行当，自己可是半点风险也不肯担的。

厨子拿出了看家本事，这顿晚餐，我相信，是十分精致的。那道奥伊大炖羹（Oille）——用鹌鹑、阉鸡、鸭子和别的好味道的肉熬成的最腴美的一道菜——堪称烹饪艺术的一绝；还有牛肉炖黄瓜浓汤、镶膘饰以白煮蛋的小牛胸肉，也同样精彩。其实这一切全是可怕的糟蹋：这么好的菜摆在几乎不肯动一箸的客人面前，看了叫人心酸。吉尔达吃不下，每回试着下咽，喉咙就像堵住了。真的，她得搬出自尊心里最后一丝残余，才能在仇人面前撑住那点体面。市长也是一样，受辱的羞愤烧红了脸，对着乖戾的扬端上来的一道道菜，也不过是浅尝辄止。

斯托滕贝格勋爵呢，却做出一副兴高采烈的样子，谈锋甚健，酒也喝得凶，还大声地、盛气凌人地同尼古拉斯议论起大公夫人（the Archduchess）征服尼德兰的种种计划。尼古拉斯灌下两三杯上头的西班牙烈酒，胆气也壮了，对吉尔达责备的目光报以冷漠，对父亲的轻蔑报以挑衅。


### II / 二

===Original===
What Gilda suffered it were a vain attempt to describe. How she contrived to remain at the table; to appear indifferent almost gay; to glance up now and again at a persuasive challenge from Stoutenburg, will forever remain her secret. She never spoke of that hour, of that hateful, harrowing supper, like an odious nightmare, which was wont in after years to sent a shudder of horror right through her whenever she recalled it.

The burgomaster remained at first obstinately silent, whilst the Lord of Stoutenburg talked with studied insolence of the future of the Netherlands. The happy times would now come back, the traitor vowed, when the United Provinces, dissolved into feeble and separate entities, without form or governance, would once more return to their allegiance and bow the knee before the might of Spain; when the wholesome rule of another Alva would teach these stiffnecked and presumptuous burghers that comfort and a measure of welfare could only be obtained by unconditional surrender and submission to a high, unconquerable Power.

“Freedom! Liberty!” he sneered. “Ancient Charters! Bah! Empty, swaggering words, I say, which their masters will soon force them to swallow. Then will follow an era more suited to all this beggarly Dutch rabble, one that will teach them a lesson which will at last stick in their memories. The hangman, that’s what they want! The stake! The rack! Our glorious Inquisition, and the relentlessness which, alas, for the nonce hath lain buried with our immortal Alva!”

===Chinese===
吉尔达受了怎样的罪，描摹她是徒劳的。她怎样撑着没有离席；怎样装出淡漠、几乎快活的样子；怎样在斯托滕贝格一次次缠人的搭讪挑衅下抬眼相迎——这一切将永远是她的秘密。那个时辰，那顿可憎的、剜心的晚餐，她后来从未提起；它像一场可厌的噩梦，多年之后，只要一回想起来，仍会叫她浑身上下掠过一阵恐怖的战栗。

市长起初一味执拗地沉默着，任斯托滕贝格勋爵拿捏着无礼的分寸，大谈尼德兰的前途。这个叛贼信誓旦旦地说，好日子就要回来了：到时候，尼德兰联省共和国分崩离析，化作一堆孱弱散乱、既无形体又无纲纪的碎块，重新归顺称臣，跪倒在西班牙的威势面前；到时候，再来一个阿尔瓦式「有益」的统治，好生教训教训这些顽固狂妄的市民——叫他们明白，安逸和些许的温饱，唯有无条件投降、匍匐在一个高不可犯、不可战胜的强权脚下，方能求得。

「自由！解放！」他讥笑道。「古老宪章！呸！我说，全是空口说大话的词儿，他们的主子很快就会逼他们把这些话吞回去。跟着就会来一个时代，更配这帮叫花子似的荷兰贱民，好好给他们一番教训，叫他们一辈子忘不掉。绞刑吏，那才是他们要的！火刑柱！拉肢刑架！还有我们光荣的宗教裁判所，还有那份狠辣——可惜啊，眼下只能跟着我们不朽的阿尔瓦一起埋在土里！」

===Original===
He drank a loyal toast to the coming new era, to the Archduchess, to King Philip IV, who in his glorious reign would see Spain once more unconquered, the Netherlands subdued, England punished at last. Nicolaes joined him with many a lustful shout, whilst the burgomaster sat with set lips, his eyes glowing with suppressed indignation. Once or twice it seemed as if his stern self-control would give way, as if his burning wrath would betray him into words and deeds that might cause abysmal misery to hundreds of innocent people whilst not serving in any way the cause which he would have given his life to uphold.

Indeed, in the book of heroic deeds of which God’s angel hath a record, none stand out more brilliantly than the endurance of the Burgomaster of Amersfoort and of his daughter on this memorable occasion. Nor is there in the whole valorous history of the Netherlands a more glorious page than that which tells of the sacrifice made by father and daughter in order to save the city which they loved from threatened annihilation.

===Chinese===
他满怀忠忱地举杯，祝即将降临的新时代，祝大公夫人，祝腓力四世国王——在他辉煌的治世里，西班牙将再度立于不败之地，尼德兰被征服，英格兰终于受到惩罚。尼古拉斯随着他大声哄闹叫好，市长却紧闭着嘴唇坐着，双眼里燃着强压下去的义愤。有一两回，他那份严酷的自制眼看就要崩溃，那股炽烈的怒火眼看就要驱使他说出、做出什么来——那也许会给千百个无辜的人招来灭顶的灾祸，却丝毫无补于他甘愿舍命维护的大义。

真的，在上帝的天使所记录的英雄事迹簿上，没有什么比阿默斯福特市长和他女儿在这个难忘时刻的坚忍更加光彩夺目。在整个尼德兰英勇的历史里，也再找不出比这更辉煌的一页——为了使他们热爱的城池免遭涂炭，父女二人做出了那样的牺牲。


### III / 三

===Original===
But like all things, good and evil, the trial came to an end at last. The Lord of Stoutenburg gave the signal, and the burgomaster and Gilda rose from the table both, in truth, with a deep sigh of thankfulness.

Stoutenburg remained deferential until the end—deferential, that is, with an undercurrent of mockery, which he took no pains to conceal. His bow, as he finally took leave of his guests, bidding the burgomaster a simple farewell and Gilda au revoir until the dawn on the morrow, was so obviously ironical that Beresteyn was goaded into an indignant tirade, which he regretted almost as soon as he had uttered it.

“Let him who stands,” he said firmly, and with all of his wonted dignity, “take heed lest he fall. The Netherlands are not conquered yet, my lord, because your mercenary troops have succeeded, for the time being, in overrunning one of her provinces. Ede may have fallen. Amersfoort may for the moment, be under your heel—”

“Arnheim and Nijmegen may have capitulated by now,” Stoutenburg broke in derisively. “Sold to De Berg, like Amersfoort and Ede, by the craven *smeerlap* to whom you have given your daughter.”

===Chinese===
然而天下事，无论善恶，总有到头的时候，这场磨难终于也挨到了尽头。斯托滕贝格勋爵示意散席，市长和吉尔达双双起身——说实话，两人心里都深深舒了一口感恩的气。

斯托滕贝格从头到尾保持着恭敬——也就是说，一种带着讥讽底子的恭敬，他也懒得掩饰。最后送客时，他对市长只道了一声寻常的再见，对吉尔达则道「明日黎明再会」（au revoir）；他那一躬如此明显地带着讥诮，气得贝雷斯坦（Beresteyn）忍不住发作了一通愤慨的长篇责骂——话一出口，他几乎立刻就后悔了。

「站着的人，」他语气坚定，拿出他素来的全部威仪，说道，「也当留神，免得跌倒。尼德兰还没有被征服，勋爵大人，哪怕你的雇佣军一时得逞，践踏了她的一个省份。埃德或许已经陷落。阿默斯福特此刻或许也在你的脚跟之下——」

「阿纳姆和奈梅亨这会儿说不定也已经开城了，」斯托滕贝格讥嘲地插嘴道，「跟阿默斯福特和埃德一样，卖给了德·贝格——经手人就是你把女儿嫁给的那个卑怯的龌龊货（*smeerlap*）。」

===Original===
“Even that may have happened,” the burgomaster riposted hotly, “if so be the will of God. But we are a race of fighters. We have beaten and humiliated the Spaniard and driven him from off our land before now. And Maurice of Nassau, the finest captain of the age, is unconquered still!”

“Mightily sick, so I’m told,” the other put in carelessly. “He was over-ready, methinks, to abandon Amersfoort to its fate.”

“Only to punish you more effectually in the end. Take heed, my lord, take heed! The multiplicity of your crimes will find you out soon enough.”

“ ’Sblood!” retorted Stoutenburg, unperturbed; “but you forget, mynheer burgomaster, that, whate’er betide me, your daughter’s fate is henceforth linked to mine own.”

===Chinese===
「就算那样的事发生了，」市长涨红着脸激烈地回敬，「那也是上帝的旨意。可我们是一个善战的种族。我们早就打败过西班牙人、羞辱过西班牙人，把他们从我们的土地上赶了出去。而拿骚的莫里斯，当世最出色的统帅，至今未曾落败！」

「病得可不轻呢，我听说，」那位满不在乎地插话。「依我看，他未免也太急于丢下阿默斯福特，任它自生自灭了。」

「那不过是为了到头来更彻底地收拾你。小心吧，勋爵大人，小心吧！你累累的罪行，很快就会找上门来。」

「见鬼！」斯托滕贝格不动声色地回敬道；「不过您忘了，市长老爷，不管我落到什么地步，令嫒的命运从此都拴在我的命运上了。」

===Original===
Then it was that Beresteyn repented of his outburst, for indeed he had gained nothing by it, and Stoutenburg had used the one argument which was bound to silence him. What, in truth, was the use of wrangling? Dignity was sure to suffer, and that mocking recreant would only feel that his triumph was more complete.

Even now he only laughed, pointed with an ironical flourish of his arm to the widely open doors, through which in the dimly lighted hall, a group of men could be perceived, sitting or standing around the centre table, with Diogenes standing in their midst, his fair head crowned by the hideous bandage, and his broad shoulders towering above the puny, swarthy Spanish soldiery. He had a mug of ale in his hand, and holding it aloft he was singing a ribald song, the refrain of which was taken up by the men. In the vague and flickering light of resin torches, his sightless orbs looked spectral, like those of a wraith.

“You should be grateful to me, mynheer,” Stoutenburg added with a sneer, “for freeing your daughter from such a yoke.”

===Chinese===
贝雷斯坦这才懊悔自己不该发作，因为这一通发作确实没讨到半分便宜，斯托滕贝格反而使出了那个注定叫他哑口无言的道理。说实话，争这个嘴有什么用？体面注定要受损失，而那个讥诮的变节者只会觉得自己的胜利更加圆满罢了。

这会儿他只是大笑，手臂讥诮地一挥，指向敞开的大门：透过门去，昏暗的大厅里可以望见一群人，围坐在长桌四周，第欧根尼立在人群当中，俊美的头颅上缠着那条骇人的绷带，宽阔的肩膀耸立在矮小黝黑的西班牙大兵之上。他手里端着一大杯麦酒，高举着，正唱一支下流的曲子，副歌一起，众人便轰然应和。松脂火把明明灭灭的光里，他那一双失明的眼珠显得鬼气森森，宛如幽魂的眼睛。

「您倒该感激我呢，老爷，」斯托滕贝格讥笑着补了一句，「我替令嫒卸下了这样一副枷锁。」

===Original===
He returned to Gilda, took her unresisting hand and raised it to his lips. Above it, he was watching her face. She was looking beyond him, straight at the blind man; and though Stoutenburg at that moment would have bartered much for the knowledge of what was in her thoughts, he could not define the expression of her eyes. At one time he thought that they had softened, that the fulfilment of all his hopes was hanging once more in the balance. It seemed for the moment as if she would snatch away her hand and seek shelter, as she had done before, against the heart of her beloved; that right through that outer husk of misery and degradation she saw something that puzzled her rather than repelled. A question seemed to be hovering on her lips. A question of a protest. Or was it a mute appeal for forgiveness?

Stoutenburg could not tell. But he felt that for a space of a few seconds the whole edifice of his desires was tottering, that Fate might, after all, still be holding a thunderbolt in store for him, which would hurl him down from the pinnacle of this momentary triumph. Gilda—as a woman—was still unconquered. Neither her heart nor her soul would ever be his. Somehow it was the glance wherewith she regarded the blind man that told the Lord of Stoutenburg this one unalterable fact.

The sortilege which he had tried to evoke, by letting her look on the pitiful wreck who had once been her lover, had fallen short in its potent charm. His own brilliant prospects, his masterful personality, ay, his well-assumed indifference, had all failed to cast their spells over her. Unlike the valiant Petruchio of the English play, he had not yet succeeded in taming this beautiful shrew. In the past she had resisted his blandishments; if she succumbed at all, it would be beneath the weight of his tyranny.

===Chinese===
他回到吉尔达身边，拿起她不曾抵抗的手，举到唇边。手上方，他那双眼正盯着她的脸。她的目光越过他，直直地落在那盲人身上；这一刻，斯托滕贝格纵然肯拿许多东西去换，也换不来她心里转的究竟是什么念头，他辨认不出她眼中的神情。有一霎他以为那眼神软化了，以为他全部希望的实现又一次悬在了天平上。那一瞬间，她仿佛就要把手抽回去，像从前那样，到她心上人的胸口去寻庇护；仿佛透过那悲惨与堕落的外壳，她看见的不是叫她憎厌的东西，倒是一样叫她困惑的东西。一个疑问似乎正浮上她的唇边。一声抗议的疑问。抑或是一句无声的、乞求宽恕的呼吁？

斯托滕贝格说不上来。但他觉出，有那么几秒钟，他欲望的整座大厦正在摇晃；命运毕竟可能还攥着一道霹雳，预备把他从这片刻胜利的峰顶劈下来。吉尔达——作为一个女人——仍未被征服。她的心、她的灵魂，永远不会归他所有。不知怎的，正是她望向那盲人的那一眼，把这件无可更改的事实告诉了斯托滕贝格勋爵。

他原想施展的那套巫术——让她亲眼看看她昔日的恋人沦落成的可怜残躯——法力终究不够灵验。他自己煊赫的前程，他凌厉的性格，还有他那副装得很像的冷漠，全都没能在她身上施起咒来。他不像英国人那出戏里勇敢的彼特鲁乔（Petruchio），至今还没能驯服这个美丽的泼妇。从前她就顶住了他的百般诱哄；她若有屈服的一天，那也只能屈服在他暴虐的重压之下。

===Original===
Well, so be it! Nicolaes, no doubt, had been right when he said that women reserved their disdain for weaklings. It was the man of iron who won a woman’s love. The thought sent a fierce glow of hatred coursing through his blood. Mythical and fatalistic as he was, he believed that his lucky star would only begin to rise when he had succeeded in winning Gilda for his own. He had deemed women an easy conquest in the past. This one could not resist him for long. Even men were wont to come readily under his way—witness Nicolaes Beresteyn, who was as wax in his hands. In the past, he had delighted in wielding a kind of cabalistic power, which he undoubtedly possessed, over many a weak or shifty character. His mother even was wont to call him a magician, and stood not a little in awe of the dark-visaged, headstrong child, and later on of the despotic, lawless youth, who had set the crown on her manifold sorrows by his callousness and his crimes.

That power had been on the wane of late. But it was not—could not—be gone from him forever. Nicolaes was still his sycophant. Jan and his kind were willing to go to death for him. His own brain had devised a means for bringing that obstinate burgomaster and the beautiful Gilda to their knees. Then, of a surety, in the Cornucopia of Fate there was something more comforting, more desirable, than a thunderbolt!

Was he not a man the master of his destiny?

===Chinese===
好吧，那就这样吧！尼古拉斯说女人只肯把轻蔑留给软骨头，这话无疑是对的。赢得女人爱情的，是铁打的男人。这个念头让一股凶悍的恨火沿着他的血液奔流。他天性迷信而宿命，相信只有把吉尔达夺到了手，他的福星才会开始升起。过去他把女人看作唾手可得的战利品。这一个也抗拒不了他多久。连男人也惯于轻易落入他的彀中——尼古拉斯·贝雷斯坦便是明证，在他手里就像一块蜡。从前，他就喜欢对种种软弱、反复的性格行使一种近乎巫术的威力——这威力他无疑确实拥有。连他母亲都常唤他作魔法师，对这黑脸膛、犟脾气的孩子怀着不小的敬畏；到后来，对这个专制横行、无法无天的青年更是如此——他用自己的凉薄和罪行，给她那百般的不幸加上了最后的冠冕。

这威力近来是衰退了。可它并没有——也不可能——永远离开他。尼古拉斯仍旧是他的谄媚之徒。扬和他那号人甘愿为他去死。他自己的脑袋已经想出了一条妙计，能让那个倔强的市长和美丽的吉尔达双双跪倒。到那时，命运的丰饶角（Cornucopia）里，定然还装着比霹雳更叫人宽心、更叫人渴求的东西！

他难道不是一个主宰自己命运的男人么？

===Original===
Bah! What was a woman’s love, after all? Why not let her go—be content with worldly triumphs? The sacking of Amersfoort, which would yield him wealth and treasure; the gratitude of the Archduchess: a high—if not the highest—position in the reconquered provinces! Why not be content with those? And Stoutenburg groaned like a baffled tiger, because in his heart of hearts he knew these things would not content him in the end. He wanted Gilda! Gilda, of the blue eyes and the golden hair, the demure glance and fragrant hands. His desire for her was in his bones, and he felt that he would indeed go raving mad if he lost her after this—if that beggarly drunkard, unwashed, dishonoured, and stricken with blindness, triumphed through his very abasement and the magnitude of his misfortune.

“This, at any rate, I can avert!” he murmured under his breath. And somehow the thought eased the racking jealousy that was torturing him—jealousy of such an abject thing. He waited until Gilda had passed out of the room, and when she was standing in the hall, so obviously bidding a last farewell in her heart to the man she loved so well, he called peremptorily to Jan:

“Take the varlet,” he commanded roughly, “and hang him on the Koppel-poort!”

===Chinese===
呸！说到底，一个女人的爱情算得了什么？何不放她去——满足于尘世间的凯旋？洗劫阿默斯福特，那会给他带来财富和珍宝；大公夫人的感激：在收复的诸省里坐上一个高位——哪怕不是至高的位置！何不满足于这些呢？斯托滕贝格却像一只受挫的老虎似的低吼起来，因为在他内心深处，他知道这些东西终究填不满他。他要的是吉尔达！蓝眼睛、金头发的吉尔达，眼波娴静、双手芳香的吉尔达。他对她的渴望刻进了骨头里；他觉得，倘若这一回再失去她——倘若那个叫花子似的醉鬼，不梳不洗、声名扫地、双目失明，竟靠着他那副潦倒相和他那场大不幸本身赢了她——他真的会发狂。

「这一层，无论如何，我还来得及挽回！」他压着嗓子喃喃自语。不知怎的，这个念头缓解了那正凌迟着他的、揪心的嫉妒——对一个如此卑贱之物的嫉妒。他等吉尔达走出房去，等她站到大厅里——明摆着正在心里同她深爱的男人诀别——才厉声唤扬：

「把那恶棍带走，」他粗暴地下令，「吊死在科佩尔门（Koppel-poort）上！」

===Original===
At the word Gilda turned on him like an infuriated tigress. Pushing past her father, past the men, who recoiled from her as if from a madwoman, she was back beside the execrable despot who thus put the crown on his hideous cruelties.

“Your bargain, my lord!” she cried hoarsely. “You dare not—you dare not—”

“My bargain, fair one?” Stoutenburg retorted coolly. “Nay, you were so averse to fulfilling your share of it, that I have repented me of proposing it. The varlet hangs. That is my last word.”

His last word! And Jan so ready to obey! The men were already closing in around her beloved; less than a minute later they had his hands securely pinioned behind his back. Can you wonder that she lost her head, that she fought to free herself from her father’s arms, and, throwing reserve, dignity to the winds, threw herself at the feet of that inhuman monster and pleaded with him as no woman on earth had, mayhap, ever pleaded before?

===Chinese===
一听这话，吉尔达像一头暴怒的母虎向他猛扑过去。她推开父亲，推开众人——那些人纷纷躲闪，仿佛躲一个疯女人——转眼又回到了那可憎的暴君身边；他方才那句话，就这样给他种种骇人的残暴加上了冠冕。

「你的交易，勋爵大人！」她嘶哑地喊道。「你不敢——你不敢——」

「我的交易，美人儿？」斯托滕贝格冷冷地回敬。「不，你那样百般不愿履行你那一半，我早后悔提出它了。这恶棍吊定了。这是我的最后一句话。」

他的最后一句话！而扬又那样急于从命！众人已经朝她心上人围拢过去；不到一分钟，他的双手已被牢牢反剪着绑在了背后。她方寸大乱，你还能奇怪么？她拼命要从父亲怀抱里挣脱出来，把矜持、把体面统统抛到了九霄云外，扑倒在那没有人性的恶魔脚下，向他哀求——哀求得那样恳切，普天之下，兴许还没有一个女人这样哀求过。

===Original===
We do not like to think of that exquisite, refined woman kneeling before such an abominable dastard. Yet she did it! Words of appeal, of entreaty, poured from her quivering lips. She raised her tear-stained face to his, embraced his knees with her arms. She forgot the men that stood by, puzzled and vaguely awed—Jan resolute, her father torn to the heart. She forgot everything save that there was a chance—a remote chance—of softening a cruel heart, and she could not—no, could not!—see the man she loved dragged to shameful death before her eyes.

She promised—oh, she promised all that she had to give!

“I’ll be your willing slave, my lord, in all things,” she pleaded, her voice broken and hoarse. “Your loving wife, as you desire. A kiss from me? Take it, an you will. I’ll not resist! Nay, I’ll return it from my heart, in exchange for your clemency.”

Then it was that the burgomaster succeeded at last in tearing her away from her humiliating position. He dragged her to her feet, drew her to his breast, tried by words and admonition to revive in her her sense of dignity and her self-control. Only with one word did he, in his turn, condescend to plead.

“An you have a spark of humanity left in you, my lord,” he said loudly, “order your executioners to be quick about their business.”

===Chinese===
我们不忍去想那样一位优雅精致的女人，竟跪在这样一个可鄙的懦夫面前。可她偏偏跪了！哀告的、恳求的话语，从她颤抖的唇间倾泻出来。她把泪痕斑斑的脸仰向他，张开双臂抱住他的双膝。她忘记了旁边站着的人——他们又困惑又隐隐生畏——扬神色决然，她父亲心肝俱裂。她什么都忘记了，只记得还有一线机会——一线渺茫的机会——能软化一颗狠毒的心；她不能——不，不能！——眼睁睁看着她爱的男人被拖去受那横死的羞辱。

她许诺了——啊，她把她所有能给的都许诺了！

「我甘愿做您顺从的奴隶，勋爵大人，事事顺从，」她哀求道，嗓音破碎而嘶哑。「做您恩爱的妻子，如您所愿。要我一吻？您要便拿去。我不抗拒！不，我还要发自心底地回您一吻，只求您开恩。」

直到这时，市长才终于把她从那受辱的境地中硬拉了起来。他把她拽起身，搂到怀里，用言语和劝诫，努力唤回她的体面感和自持。而他自己，也只肯降低身价，用一个词向他求告。

「只要你心里还剩着一星半点的人性，勋爵大人，」他朗声说道，「就吩咐你手下的刽子手，叫他们把差事办得利索些吧。」

===Original===
For the Lord of Stoutenburg had, with a refinement of cruelty almost unbelievable, were it not a matter of history, stayed Jan from executing his inhuman order.

“Wait!” his glittering eyes appeared to say to the sycophant henchman who hung upon his looks. “Let me enjoy this feast until I am satiated.”

Then, when Gilda lay at last, half-swooning in the shelter of her father’s arms, he said coolly:

“Have I not said, fair one, that if you deigned to plead the rascal should not hang? See! The potency of your charm upon my sensitive heart! The man who hath always been my most bitter enemy, and whom at last I have within my power, shall live because your fair arms did encircle my knees, and because of your free will you offered me a kiss. Mynheer Burgomaster,” he added, with easy condescension, “I pray you lead your daughter to her room. She is overwrought and hath need of rest. Go in peace, I pray you. That drunken varlet is safe now in my hands.”

===Chinese===
原来斯托滕贝格勋爵以一种几乎令人难以置信的精致的残忍——若非史有其事，简直叫人无法相信——早已拦住扬，不许他即刻执行那道惨无人道的命令。

「等着！」他那一双灼灼的眼睛，仿佛在对那个仰他脸色行事的谄媚爪牙这样说。「让我把这场盛宴享受到饱。」

随后，等吉尔达终于半昏半醒地瘫在父亲怀抱的庇护里，他才冷冷地说：

「我不是说过么，美人儿，只要你肯屈尊开口求告，那恶棍就不吊了？瞧！你的魅力在我这颗敏感的心上有多大神通！这个一向是我死敌、终于落进我手心的人，可以活了——因为你那美丽的双臂搂过我的双膝，也因为你心甘情愿地许了我一吻。市长老爷，」他带着从容的俯就补了一句，「请您领令嫒回房去吧。她方才太激动，需要歇息。请安心去吧。那个醉鬼恶棍，如今稳稳在我掌中了。」

===Original===
The burgomaster could not trust himself to reply. Only his loving hands wandered with a gentle, soothing gesture over his beloved daughter’s hair, whilst he murmured soft, endearing words in her ear. Gradually she became more calm, was able to gather her wits together, to realize what she had done and all that she had sacrificed, probably in vain. Stoutenburg had spoken soft words, but how could she trust him, who had ever proved himself a liar and a cheat? She was indeed like a miserable, captive bird, held, maimed and bruised, in a cruel trap set by vengeful and cunning hands. It seemed almost incredible why she should be made to suffer so.

What had she done? In what horrible way had she sinned before God, that His hand should lie so heavily upon her? Even her sacrifice—sublime and selfless—failed to give her the consolation of duty nobly accomplished. Everything before her was dreary and dark. Life itself was nought but torture. The few days—hours—that must intervene until she knew that Amersfoort was safe confronted her like the dark passage into Gehenna. Beyond them lay death at last, and she, a young girl scarce out of adolescence, hitherto rich, beautiful, adulated, was left to long for that happy release from misery with an intensity of longing akin to the sighing of souls in torment.

===Chinese===
市长不敢让自己开口回答。只有他慈爱的双手，带着轻柔、抚慰的动作，缓缓拂过爱女的头发，口中向她低唤着温柔的疼惜话。她渐渐平静下来，能收拢神智了，明白自己做下了什么，明白自己牺牲了一切——多半是白白牺牲。斯托滕贝格说了些软话，可她怎能信他？他从来就证明自己是个说谎的、坑人的骗子。她真的像一只可怜的笼中鸟，带伤带瘀，被一双怀恨而狡诈的手扣在残酷的陷阱里。她何苦要受这样的罪，想想简直叫人难以置信。

她做下了什么？她在上帝面前造了什么可怕的孽，他的手才这样沉重地压在她身上？就连她的牺牲——崇高而无私的牺牲——也没能给她一丝「已尽天职」的安慰。她眼前的一切凄凉而黑暗。生命本身不过是刑罚。要等上几天——几个时辰——才能知道阿默斯福特是否平安；那段时光横在她面前，像一条通向地狱（Gehenna）的黑暗甬道。甬道的尽头终于是死，而她，一个刚刚走出花季的少女，向来富有、美丽、受人奉承，如今只能渴盼那从苦难中解脱的幸福的归宿——那份渴盼之切，已近于受难灵魂的叹息。

## XIV Treachery / XIV 变节

### I / 一

===Original===
Throughout this harrowing scene the blind man had stood by, pinioned, helpless, almost lifeless in his immobility. The only sign of life in him seemed to be in those weird, sightless orbs, in which the flickering light of the resin torches appeared to draw shafts of an unearthly glow. He was pinioned and could not move. Half a dozen soldiers had closed in around him. Whether he heard all that went on, many who were there at the time declared it to be doubtful. But, even if he heard, what could he have done? He could not even put his hands up to his ears to shut out that awful sound of his beloved wife’s hoarse, spent voice pleading desperately for him.

One of the men who was on guard over him told De Voocht afterwards that he could hear the tough sinews cracking against the bonds that held the giant captive, and that great drops of sweat appeared upon the fine, wide brow. When Gilda, leaning heavily upon her father’s arm, finally mounted the stairs which led up to her room, the blind man turned his head in that direction. But the jongejuffrouw went on with head bent and did not glance down in response.

All this we know from De Voocht, who speaks of it in his “Brieven.” But he was not himself present on the scene and hath it only from hearsay. He questioned several of the men subsequently as he came in contact with them, and, of course, the burgomaster’s testimony was the most clear and the most detailed. Mynheer Beresteyn admitted that, throughout that awful, ne’er-to-be-forgotten evening, he could not understand the blind man’s attitude, was literally tortured with doubts of him. Was he, in truth, the craven wretch which he appeared to be﻿—the miserable traitor who had sold the Stadtholder’s original plans to De Berg, betrayed Marquet and De Keysere, and hopelessly jeopardized the whole of Gelderland, if not the entire future of the Netherlands? If so, he was well-deserving of the gallows, which would not fail to be his lot.

===Chinese===
在这剜心摧肺的一幕里，盲人始终立在一旁，被捆绑着，动弹不得，僵直得几乎没了生气。他身上仿佛唯一还活着的，只剩那双怪异的、看不见东西的眼珠——松脂火把摇曳的光，仿佛在其中抽出一道道幽异的辉芒。他被反绑着，动不得。五六个士兵紧紧围住他。他究竟听没听见周围发生的一切，当时在场的许多人后来都表示怀疑。可是，纵然听见了，他又还能做什么呢？他连抬手掩住耳朵都办不到，没法把那可怕的声音挡在外面——那是他心爱的妻子，用嘶哑衰竭的嗓子，拼命为他哀求的声音。

看守他的一名士兵后来告诉德·福赫特（De Voocht），说他听得见这个被缚的巨汉身上紧韧的筋腱绷着绳索咯咯作响，还看得见那光洁宽阔的额头上渗出豆大的汗珠。当吉尔达（Gilda）把全身的重量都压在父亲手臂上、终于登上通往她闺房的楼梯时，盲人把头朝那个方向转了过去。可小姐只顾低头往前走，没有抬眼回望。

这一切我们都得自德·福赫特，他在《书简》（Brieven）里谈到了这件事。但他本人当时并不在场，不过是得诸传闻。事后他陆续接触到几名士兵，一一询问过；自然，其中数市长（burgomaster）的证述最为清晰、最为详尽。贝雷斯坦老爷（Mynheer Beresteyn）承认，那个可怕的、永难忘怀的夜晚自始至终，他都参不透盲人的态度，简直被心头的疑虑折磨透了。他当真就是他看上去的那副懦怯胚子——把执政（the Stadtholder）的原定计划出卖给德·贝格（De Berg）、背叛了马凯（Marquet）与德·凯泽雷（De Keysere）、把整个海尔德兰乃至尼德兰的全部前程都断送得干干净净的可鄙叛徒吗？倘若果真如此，那他上绞架实属罪有应得，而这绞架也注定是他的下场。

===Original===
But was he? Was he?

The face, of course, out of which the light of the eyes had vanished, was inscrutable. The mouth, remember, was partially hidden by the three days’ growth of beard, and grime and fatigue had further obliterated all other marks of expression. Of course, the man must have suffered tortures of humiliation and rage, which would effectually deaden all physical pain. But at the time he seemed not to suffer. Indeed, at one moment it almost seemed if he were asleep, with sightless eyes wide open, and standing on his feet.

===Chinese===
可他当真是吗？当真是吗？

那张脸自然叫人无从索解——眼里的光芒早已熄灭。还有，别忘了，嘴巴半掩在三天未剃的胡须里，污垢与疲惫又把其余种种表情的痕迹抹得干干净净。当然，此人心里必定受着屈辱与狂怒的煎熬，那样的煎熬足以麻木一切肉体的疼痛。可当时他仿佛并不痛苦。的确，有一阵子他看上去简直像是睡着了——一双失明的眼睛大睁着，人却直挺挺地立在那里。

### II / 二

===Original===
After Gilda and her father had disappeared on the floor above, the Lord of Stoutenburg, like a wild and caged beast awaiting satisfaction, began pacing up and down the long banqueting-hall. The doors leading into it from the hall had been left wide open, and the men could see his lordship in his restless wanderings, his heavy boots ringing against the reed-covered floor. He held his arms folded across his chest, and was gnawing﻿—yes, gnawing﻿—his knuckles in the excess of his excitement and his choler.

Then he called Jan, and parleyed with him for awhile, consulted Mynheer Nicolaes, who was more taciturn and gloomy than ever before.

The soldiers knew what was coming. They had witnessed the scene between the jongejuffrouw and his Magnificence and some of them who had wives and sweethearts of their own, had felt uncomfortable lumps, at the time, in their throats. Others, who had sons, fell to wishing that their offsprings might be as finely built, as powerful as that poor, blind, intoxicated wretch who, in truth, now had no use for his magnificent muscles.

But what would you? These were troublous times. Life was cheap﻿—counted for nothing in sight of such great gentlemen as was the Lord of Stoutenburg. The varlet, it seems, had offended his lordship awhile ago. Jan knew the story, and was very bitter about it, too. Well, no man could expected to be treated with gentleness by a great lord whom he had been fool enough to offend. The blind rascallion would hang, of that there could be no doubt. The jongejuffrouw had been pacified with soft words and vague promises, but the rascal would hang. Any man there would have bet his shirt on the issue. You had only to look at his lordship. A more determined, more terrifying look it were impossible to meet. Even Jan looked a little scared. When his Magnificence looked like that it boded no good to anyone. All the rancour, the gall, that had accumulated in his heart against everything that pertained to the United Provinces and to their Stadtholder would effectively smother the slightest stirring of conscience or pity. Perhaps, when the jongejuffrouw knelt at his feet, he had thought of his mother, who, equally distraught and equally humiliated, had knelt in vain at the Stadtholder’s feet, pleading for the life of her sons. Oh, yes, all that had made the Lord of Stoutenburg terribly hard and callous.

===Chinese===
吉尔达和父亲在楼上消失之后，斯托滕贝格勋爵（The Lord of Stoutenburg）便像一头关在笼中等着餍足的野兽，在长长的宴会厅里来回踱起步来。从大厅通往宴会厅的门大敞着，士兵们看得见他大人焦躁地转来转去，沉重的马靴踏得铺灯芯草的地板咚咚作响。他双臂抱在胸前，激动与恼怒到了极处，竟咬起——一点不错，咬起——自己的指节来。

随后他唤来扬（Jan），同他计议了一阵，又征询尼古拉斯老爷（Mynheer Nicolaes）的意见——这位比以往任何时候都更沉默、更阴郁。

士兵们心里明白将要发生什么。他们目睹了小姐与他大人阁下之间的那一幕，其中一些自家有妻室、有意中人的，当时都觉得喉咙里哽了一团东西，好不受用。另一些有儿子的，则不禁盼着自家的儿子也能长得像那个可怜的、失明的醉鬼那样筋骨匀称、那样魁伟——虽说那身好筋肉，如今对他实在已没什么用处了。

可是你又有什么办法呢？时世就是如此动荡。人命不值钱——在斯托滕贝格勋爵这样的大贵人眼里，压根算不得什么。据说那恶棍不久前冲撞了他大人。扬知道这段过节，也为这事怀恨在心。唉，一个人既然蠢到冲撞了一位大贵人，就别指望人家对他手下留情。这瞎眼的无赖准得上绞架，这没什么可怀疑的。小姐已经被温言软语和含糊的许诺安抚住了，可这无赖准得上绞架。在场随便哪个都敢拿身上的衬衫打这个赌。只消看看他大人的脸色就够了。再坚决、再骇人的神色，你休想见到第二个。连扬都有点发怵。他大人阁下一露出那副神色，对谁都不会有好事。他对尼德兰联省共和国及其执政所积下的全部怨毒、全部苦恨，足以把良心或怜悯最轻微的悸动都闷死。也许，方才小姐跪在他脚下时，他想起了自己的母亲——那个同样心碎、同样受辱，曾在执政脚下白白跪求儿子们活命的母亲。噢，是的，这一切早把斯托滕贝格勋爵磨砺得铁石心肠、冷酷无情了。

===Original===
But the men were sorry for the blind vagabond, for all that. He had had nothing to do with the feuds between the Stadtholder and the sons of Olden Barneveldt. He had done nothing, seemingly, save to win the love of the beautiful lady whom his Magnificence had marked for his own. He was brave, too. You could not help admiring him as he stood between you and your comrades, his head thrown back, a splendid type of virility and manhood. Half-seas over he may have been. His misfortunes were, in truth, enough to make any man take a drink; but you could not help but see that there was an air of spirituality about the forehead and the sensitive nostrils which redeemed the face from any suggestion of sensuality. And now and again a quaint smile would play round the corners of his mouth, and the whole wan face would light up as if with a sudden whimsical thought.

Then all at once he threw back his head and yawned.

Such a droll fellow! Yawning on the brink of eternity! It was, in truth, a pity he should hang!

===Chinese===
不过尽管如此，士兵们还是怜惜那个瞎眼的流浪汉。执政与奥尔登巴内费尔特（Olden Barneveldt）诸子之间的恩怨，本与他毫不相干。他没做过什么，看样子，不过是赢得了那位美丽夫人的爱情——而他大人阁下早把这位夫人视作自己的禁脔。他也很勇敢。他站在你和你的伙伴们中间，昂着头，那是一副何等出色的血性男儿的仪表，叫人不能不佩服。他也许是醉意已有七八分了。说真的，他那些遭际，够叫任何人借酒浇愁的了；可你还是不能不看到，他的额头和那敏感的鼻翼间透着一股灵秀之气，使这张脸全无半点酒色之相。而且他嘴角时时会浮起一丝古怪的笑意，整张苍白的脸便倏然一亮，仿佛掠过一个突发的奇想。

忽然间，他猛地把头一仰，打了个哈欠。

多滑稽的家伙！站在永恒的边缘上打哈欠！说真的，叫这样的人上绞架，实在可惜！

### III / 三

===Original===
Yes, the blind man yawned, loudly and long, like one who is ready for bed. And the harmless sound completed Stoutenburg’s exasperation. He once more gave the harsh word of command:
