The whole thing, when accomplished, when he—Stoutenburg—was made master of Gelderland, the Stadtholder a prisoner in his hands, the United Provinces ready to submit to him, would be a revelation to her—a revelation which would make her, he doubted not, a proud and happy woman, rather than a mere obedient slave.
===Chinese===
他顿住话头，细细端详她的脸色，心里疑惑：那个醉醺醺的蠢汉——她旧日的情人，如今正死在楼下的房间里，死于一位蒙羞的父亲、一位义愤的爱国者复仇之手——这些她知道多少。可她神色那般安详，他于是断定她一无所知。市长显然有意暂且让她避开这又一重恐怖与羞辱。

也罢，无疑这样再好不过。她肯随他走了，这终究是头一件要紧事。反正另一条路是什么光景，她是知道的。

「扬留在这儿，」他说，「统带部队。我不给他传话，他不会撤离。」

在那之前，阿默斯福特全城百姓的身家性命都悬着呢。他一提这一点，她眼中便掠过一丝惊惧，单这个便足以让他确信：她全然明白自己的处境。至于执政的计划——也就是告密者出卖的那些——她自然一无所知。这样倒好，他想。等一切办成，等他——斯托滕贝格——做了海尔德兰的主人，执政成了他掌中的囚徒，联省俯首听命于他，那时自会让她大开眼界——他毫不怀疑，那一番揭幕将使她成为一个骄傲幸福的女人，而不再只是一个俯首帖耳的奴隶。

### II / 二

===Original===
In the meanwhile, he had strictly enjoined Jan to leave the banqueting hall undisturbed.

“Let the locked door and close shutters guard the grim secret within,” he said decisively. “Apparently the Heer Burgomaster intends for the nonce to hold his tongue.”

In the hurry and excitement of the departure, the soldiers, who in the night had been roused by the pistol shot, forgot that unimportant event. Certain it is that not one of them did more than cursorily wonder what it had been about. Then, as no one gave reply, the matter was soon allowed to fall into oblivion. At one moment, Stoutenburg who was standing in the hall waiting for Gilda, felt tempted to go and have a last look on his dead enemy; but the key was not in the lock and he would not send to the burgomaster for it.

It was better so.
===Chinese===
这其间，他已严令扬不得惊动宴会厅。

「就让锁死的门和关严的百叶窗，替里面守住那可怕的秘密吧。」他决然说道。「看来市长大人眼下是打算三缄其口了。」

出发时人忙马乱、群情汹汹，夜里曾被那一声枪响惊醒的士兵，早把那桩无关紧要的小事忘了。诚然，他们当中没有谁对那枪响的缘由多想过一下；既然无人作答，这事很快便被丢在了脑后。有一刻，正立在厅堂里等候吉尔达的斯托滕贝格，动了念头想再去最后看一眼死去的仇敌；可钥匙不在锁眼里，他又不愿打发人向市长去讨。

这样倒也好。

===Original===
Just then Gilda came down the stairs. She was accompanied by her old waiting woman, Maria, and was wrapped in fur cloak and hood ready for the journey. Apparently she had taken final leave of her father, and had quite resigned herself to parting from him.

“The burgomaster is well, I trust, this morning?” Stoutenburg asked with great urbanity, as soon as he had formally greeted her.

“I thank you, my lord,” she replied coolly. “My father is as well as I can desire.”

The litter was her own. Oft had she travelled in it between Haarlem and Amersfoort, when the weather was too rough for riding. Those had been happy journeys to and fro, for both homes were dear to her. Both now had become hallowed through the presence in them of her beloved. To Stoutenburg, who watched her keenly while she crossed the hall, it seemed as if once she glanced round in the direction of the banqueting room, and craned her neck as if trying to catch whatever faint sound might be coming from there. She appeared to shiver, and drew her fur cloak closer round her shoulders, her lips moved slightly as if murmuring. Stoutenburg thought that she was bidding a last farewell to the man who she could not bring herself to forget or to despise and an acute feeling of unbridled jealousy shot through him like a poisoned dart—jealousy even of the dead.
===Chinese===
正这时，吉尔达下楼来了。老侍女玛丽亚（Maria）陪着她；她裹着皮裘斗篷、戴着兜帽，已是整装待发。看样子，她已同父亲诀别过了，也已安命，就此与他分离。

「市长今晨安康吧，我希望？」正式见礼之后，斯托滕贝格极为客气地问道。

「谢谢您，勋爵大人，」她淡然答道。「家父安好，再好不过了。」

这顶轿舆本是她自己的。从前天气恶劣、不宜乘马时，她常坐着它往返于哈勒姆与阿默斯福特之间。那一趟趟来回都是快乐的旅程，因为两个家于她都是可亲的。而如今，两处宅子都因心上人在其中驻留过而成了圣地。斯托滕贝格目光锐利地望着她穿过厅堂，只觉她仿佛朝宴会厅的方向回望了一眼，伸着脖颈，像要捕捉从那边透出的哪怕最微弱的声响。她似乎打了个寒噤，把皮裘往肩头拢紧了些，嘴唇微微翕动，似在喃喃低语。斯托滕贝格心想，她这是在向那个她始终忘不掉、也鄙夷不起来的男人作最后的告别；一股狂纵的醋意像一支毒镖穿透了他——连死人他也要嫉妒。

### III / 三

===Original===
A mounted scout led the way, to clear the road of encumbrance that might retard progress. After him came the standard-bearer. Twelve Spanish halberdiers followed, the shafts of their halberts swathed in black velvet, behind them one hundred cavaliers, who were armed with muskets, and a hundred more carrying lances. Then came the litter, which was covered in leather with richly stamped leather curtains, at the sides, the shafts, front and back, supported by heavy Flemish horses, which were sumptuously caparisoned and plumed. The Lord of Stoutenburg rode on one side of the litter and Nicolaes on the other, and behind it came two more companies of musketeers and lancers.

The way lay through the Koppel-poort and then straight across the Veluwe, on the road which runs to the north of Amersfoort, thus avoiding any possible encounter with the Stadtholder’s vedettes. Stoutenburg’s intention was to await Maurice of Nassau’s coming at the molen, not to offer him battle in the open.

The road was lonely at this early hour, and a cutting wind blew across from the Zuider Zee, chasing the morning mist before it. Already on the horizon above the undulating tableland, the pale wintry sun tinged that mist with gold. Stoutenburg’s keen hawklike eyes searched the distance before him as he rode.
===Chinese===
一名骑马的探子在前面开路，清除道上一应可能耽搁行程的障碍。他后面是旗手。随后是十二名西班牙戟兵，戟杆裹着黑天鹅绒；再后面是一百名持火枪的骑兵，另有一百名擎长枪。然后才是那顶轿舆，舆身蒙着皮革，两侧挂着压花富丽的皮幔，前后舆杆由几匹健壮的佛兰德重马驾着，马披华饰，冠插羽翎。斯托滕贝格勋爵骑马行在轿舆一侧，尼古拉斯行在另一侧，舆后还跟着两队火枪兵与长枪骑兵。

一行人出了科佩尔门，随即径直穿过费吕韦，走的是阿默斯福特以北的那条大路，如此便避开了与执政哨探遭遇的一切可能。斯托滕贝格的打算，是在磨坊坐候拿骚的莫里斯前来，而不是在旷野上同他堂堂对阵。

这般早的时辰，路上寂寥无人，从须德海那面横扫过来一阵砭骨的劲风，把晨雾赶在它前面。起伏的台原之上，天边一轮苍白的冬日，已给雾气镀上淡淡金色。斯托滕贝格纵马而行，一双鹰隼似的锐眼搜索着前方远处。

===Original===
A little after seven o’clock, Barneveld was reached, and a brief halt called outside the city whilst the scouts went in, in search of provisions. The inhabitants, scared by the advent of these strangers, submitted to being fleeced of their goods, not daring to resist. Though closely questioned, they had but little information to impart. They had, in truth, heard that Ede was in the hands of the Spaniards and that Amersfoort had shared the like fate. Runners had brought the news, which was authentic, together with many wild rumours that had terrorized the credulous and paved the way for Stoutenburg’s arrival. His sable standard, with its grim device, completed the subjugation of the worthy burghers of Barneveld, who, with no garrison to protect them, thought it wisest to obey the behests of His Magnificence with a show of goodwill, rather than see their little city pillaged or their citizens dragged as captives in the train of the conqueror.

Gilda did not leave her litter during the halt. Maria, who had been riding on a pillion behind one of the equerries, who she roundly trounced and anathematized all the way, came and waited on her mistress. But Stoutenburg and Nicolaes kept with unwonted discretion, or mayhap indifference, out of her way.

The halt, in truth, lasted less than a couple of hours. By nine o’clock the troop was once more on the way, and an hour later on the high upland, out toward the east, the lonely molen loomed, portentous and weird, out of the mist.
===Chinese===
七点刚过，队伍抵达巴讷费尔德，在城外传令稍驻，探子们进城采买粮秣。这些居民被不速之客的降临吓破了胆，任凭人家把财物搜刮了去，不敢反抗。任凭怎样盘问，他们也实在说不出多少消息。他们确曾听说，埃德已落入西班牙人之手，阿默斯福特也遭了同样的命运。探报的信差带来了这个千真万确的消息，还带来许多骇人的谣传，把轻信的百姓吓得魂不附体，也为斯托滕贝格的到来铺平了道路。他那一面黑旗，连同旗上狰狞的徽记，彻底镇服了巴讷费尔德那些本分的市民：城中没有驻军保护他们，他们觉得最稳妥的，还是装出几分善意，顺从大人阁下的号令，总强过眼看着小城遭劫、市民被掳，拴在征服者的队伍后头当俘虏。

驻留期间，吉尔达没有离开轿舆。玛丽亚原本坐在一名侍从武官身后的鞍垫上，一路上把那人痛痛快快骂了个遍、咒了个透，这时过来伺候自家小姐。斯托滕贝格和尼古拉斯则一反常态地识趣——或者说是冷淡——远远避开了她。

这一场驻留，其实不足两个钟头。到九点钟，队伍重新上路；一个钟头之后，在高高的台地上，朝东一面，那座孤零零的磨坊（molen）从雾气中隐隐浮现，森然可怖，怪异不祥。

### IV / 四

===Original===
The spies of the Stadtholder, who had, according to Diogenes’ statement, spoken of the molen as Stoutenburg’s camp, where he had secreted great stores of arms and ammunition, had in truth been either deceived or deceivers.

The molen was lonely and uninhabited, as it had always been. No sign of life appeared around it, or sign of the recent breaking of a camp. True, here and there upon the scrub in the open, the scorched rough grass or a heap of ashes, indicated that a fire had been lit there at one time; whilst under the overhanging platform, the trampled earth converted into mud, and certain debris of straw and fodder, accused the recent presence of horses and of men.

But only a few. As to whether the stores of arms and ammunition were indeed concealed inside the millhouse itself, it was impossible to say from the mere aspect of the tumbledown building. Whatever secret the molen contained, it had succeeded in guarding inviolate up to this hour.
===Chinese===
执政的探子们——据第欧根尼（Diogenes）的说法，他们曾把这座磨坊说成斯托滕贝格的营地，说他在此秘藏了大批兵器弹药——其实不是受了蒙蔽，便是存心骗人。

磨坊孤零零的，无人居住，一如它素来的模样。四围不见一点生命的迹象，也不见近日拔营的痕迹。诚然，旷野的灌木丛间东一处西一处，烧焦的荒草或一堆灰烬，表明那里曾一度生过火；而外悬的平台底下，踩烂成泥的地面和些许干草饲料的残屑，则指证着马匹与人不久前在此驻留。

但那只是寥寥数人。至于兵器弹药可当真藏在磨坊屋内，单看那座破败倾颓的建筑的外观，却无从说起。这座磨坊里无论藏着什么秘密，直到此时此刻，它都守得完好无缺。

===Original===
Standing as it did upon a high point of the arid upland, the molen dominated the Veluwe. Toward the west, whence the Stadtholder would come, a gentle, undulating slope led down to Barneveld and Ede, Amersfoort and Utrecht; but in the rear of the building toward the east, the ground fell away more abruptly, down to a narrow gorge below.

It was in this gorge, secluded from the prying eyes of possible vedettes, that Stoutenburg had put up his camp ere he embarked upon his fateful expedition to Amersfoort, and it was here that he disposed the bulk of his troop: horses, men and baggage, under the command of Nicolaes Beresteyn; whilst he himself, with a bodyguard of fifty picked men, took up his quarters in the molen.

The plan of action was simple enough. The fifty men would remain concealed in and about the building, until the Stadtholder thinking the place deserted, walked straight into the trap that had been laid for him. Then, at the first musket shot, the men from the camp below were to rush up the sloping ground with a great clatter and much shouting and battle cry.
===Chinese===
磨坊矗立在干旱台地的高处，俯临整个费吕韦。西面——执政将从那面到来——一道平缓起伏的长坡逶迤而下，直通巴讷费尔德与埃德、阿默斯福特与乌得勒支；而在屋后东面，地面陡然下跌，直落到下方一道窄窄的峡沟里。

就在这道峡沟里——避开可能出现的哨探窥伺的目光——斯托滕贝格在踏上那场关系命运的阿默斯福特远征之前扎下了营盘；也是在这里，他安置了麾下人马的主力：马匹、兵员与辎重，统归尼古拉斯·贝雷斯坦掌管；他自己则带着五十名精选的卫士，进驻磨坊。

行动的方略再简单不过。那五十人埋伏在磨坊内外，只等执政以为此地空无一人，一头撞进为他设好的陷阱。然后，第一声火枪响处，山下营盘里的人马便要敲打着、呐喊着、呼着战斗口号，冲上斜坡来。

===Original===
The Stadtholder’s troops wholly unprepared for the attack would be thrown into dire confusion, and in the panic that would inevitably ensue, the rout would be complete. Stoutenburg himself would see to it that the Stadtholder did not escape.

“Welcome home, *myn engel*!” had been his semi-ironical, wholly triumphant greeting to Gilda when her litter came to a halt and he dismounted in order to conduct her into the molen.

She gave him no answer, but allowed her hand to rest in his and walked beside him with a firm step through the narrow door which gave on the interior of the millhouse. She looked about her with inquiring eyes that had not a vestige of terror in them. Almost, it seemed, at one moment as if she smiled.
===Chinese===
执政的部队对这场突袭毫无防备，必然陷于大乱；接踵而至的慌乱既无可避免，败溃便注定了。至于执政本人休想脱身，斯托滕贝格自会亲自料理。

轿舆停稳、他下马要引吉尔达进磨坊时，他迎她的那句问候——半带讥诮、十足得意——便是：「欢迎回家，我的天使（*myn engel*）！」

她没有答话，只任凭手留在他掌中，迈着坚定的步子，与他并肩穿过那扇通往磨坊内部的窄门。她一双探询的眼睛环顾四壁，其中没有一丝恐惧的影子。有一瞬间，她几乎像是微笑了。

===Original===
Did her memory conjure back just then the vision of that other molen, the one at Ryswick, where so much had happened three short months ago, and where this arrogant tyrant had played such a sorry role? Perhaps. Certain it is that she turned to him without any defiance, almost with a gentle air of appeal.

“I am very tired,” she said, with a weary little sigh, “and would be grateful for a little privacy, if your lordship would allow my tire-woman to attend on me.”

“Your wishes are my laws, *myn schat*,” he replied airily. “I entreat you to look on this somewhat dilapidated building only as a temporary halt, where nothing, alas! can be done for your comfort. I trust you will not suffer from the cold, but absolute privacy you shall have. The loft up those narrow steps is entirely at your disposal, and your woman shall come to you immediately.”

Indeed, he called at once through the door, and a moment or two later Maria appeared, reduced to silence for the nonce by a wholesome fear. Stoutenburg, in the meanwhile, still with that same ironical gallantry, had conducted Gilda to the narrow, ladder-like steps which led up to the loft. He stood at the foot, watching her serene and leisurely progress.
===Chinese===
就在那一刻，她的记忆可曾唤回另一座磨坊的光景——赖斯韦克（Ryswick）的那一座？短短三个月前，多少大事在那里发生，这位骄横的僭主又在那里扮演过一个多么不堪的角色！也许吧。可以肯定的是，她转向他时毫无倔抗之意，倒几乎带着一点柔婉恳求的神气。

「我很累了，」她带着一声倦怠的轻叹说道，「若蒙勋爵大人恩准我的梳妆女仆前来伺候，容我略得清静，感激不尽。」

「您的愿望就是我的法度，我的宝贝（*myn schat*），」他轻快地答道。「我恳请您把这座略嫌破败的房子，只当作暂时的歇脚处，只可惜在这儿，唉！没法为您添什么舒适。我相信您不至于受寒，但绝对的清静您是有的。那道窄梯上面的阁楼全归您使用，您的侍女即刻就来。」

果然，他当即隔着门唤人，不一会儿玛丽亚便来了，一时被那份有益的敬畏慑得哑然无声。这其间，斯托滕贝格依旧带着那副讥诮的殷勤，把吉尔达送到通向阁楼的那道窄而如梯的踏级前。他立在梯脚，望着她安详从容地拾级而上。

===Original===
“How wise you are, mejuffrouw,” he said, with a sigh of satisfaction. “And withal how desirable!”

She turned for a second, then, and looked down on him. But her eyes were quite inscrutable. Never had he desired her so much as now. With the gloomy background of those rickety walls behind her, she looked like an exquisite fairy; her dainty head wrapped in a hood, through which her small, oval face appeared, slightly rose-tinted, like a piece of delicate china.

The huge fur coat concealed the lines of her graceful figure, but one perfect hand rested upon the rail, and the other peeped out like a flower between the folds of her cloak. He all but lost his head when he gazed on her, and met those blue eyes that still held a mystery for him. But, with Stoutenburg, ambition and selfishness always waged successful warfare even against passion, and at this hour his entire destiny was hanging in the balance.

The look wherewith he regarded her was that of a conqueror rather than a lover. The title of the English play had come swiftly through his mind: *The Taming of the Shrew*. In truth, Nicolaes had been right. Women have no use for weaklings. It is their master whom they worship.
===Chinese===
「您多么有见识啊，小姐（mejuffrouw），」他满意地叹道。「又多么动人心魄！」

她一时回身，朝下望了他一眼。可她那双眼睛深不可测。他从没有像此刻这样渴慕过她。摇摇欲坠的墙壁阴沉沉衬在她身后，她看上去像一个精致的精灵；秀美的头颅裹在兜帽里，帽檐间露出那张小小的鹅蛋脸，微微泛着玫瑰色，像一件精美的细瓷。

宽大的皮裘掩住了她窈窕身段的线条，可一只完美的手搭在扶栏上，另一只像一朵花，从斗篷的褶裥间探出来。他凝望着她，迎着那双至今仍藏着谜团的蓝眼睛，几乎失魂。但在斯托滕贝格身上，野心与私欲纵然对阵情热，也从未打过败仗，而此时此刻，他整个的命运正悬于一线。

他注视她的那种眼神，与其说是情人，不如说是征服者。那出英国戏的戏名倏地掠过他的心头：《驯悍记》（*The Taming of the Shrew*）。说真的，尼古拉斯没有说错。女人要软弱之辈没有用。她们崇拜的，是能做她们主人的男人。

===Original===
Just one word of warning did he give her ere she finally passed out of his sight.

“There will be noise of fighting anon, *myn engel*,” he said carelessly. “Nothing that need alarm you. An encounter with vedettes probably. A few musket shots. You will not be afraid?”

“No,” she replied simply. “I will not be afraid.”

“You will be safe here with me until we can continue our journey east or south. It will depend on what progress de Berg has been able to make.”
===Chinese===
在她终于走出他的视野之前，他只给了她一句告诫。

「待会儿会有厮杀的声响，我的天使，」他漫不经心地说。「没什么好惊慌的。多半是同哨探遭遇。放几枪罢了。您不会害怕吧？」

「不会，」她简单地答道。「我不害怕。」

「您同我待在这里，安然无恙，直到我们能继续上路，往东或往南。那要看德·贝格（de Berg）打进展得如何了。」

===Original===
She gave a slight nod of understanding.

“I shall be ready,” she said.

Encouraged by her gentleness, he went on more warmly:

“And at the hour when we leave here together, *myn schat*, a runner will speed to Amersfoort with order to Jan to evacuate the city. The burgomaster will be in a position to announce to his fellow-citizens that they have nothing to fear from a chivalrous enemy, who will respect person and property, and who will go out of the gates of Amersfoort as empty-handed as he came.”
===Chinese===
她微微颔首，表示理会。

「我会准备好的。」她说。

受了她这份温顺的鼓舞，他说得更热切了：

「等到你我一同离开此地的时辰，我的宝贝，自会有一名信使飞马赶往阿默斯福特，传令扬撤出城去。市长届时便可告谕全城同胞：面对一位侠义的敌人，他们无可畏惧；这敌人尊重人身与财产，出阿默斯福特城门时，会像来时一样两手空空。」

===Original===
Whereupon he made her a low and respectful bow, stood aside to allow the serving woman to follow her mistress. Gilda had acknowledged his last pompous tirade with a faintly murmured, “I thank you, my lord.” Then she went quickly up the steps and finally passed out of his sight on the floor above.

Just for a little while he remained quite still, listening to her footsteps overhead. His lean, sharp-featured face expressed nothing but contentment now. Success—complete, absolute—was his at last! Less than a fortnight ago, he was nothing but a disappointed vagrant, without home, kindred, or prospects; scorned by the woman he loved; despised by a successful rival; an outcast from the land of his birth.

Today, his rival was dead—an object of contempt, not even of pity, for every honest man; while Gilda, like a ripe and luscious fruit, was ready to fall into his arms. And he had his foot firmly planted on the steps of a throne.
===Chinese===
说罢，他朝她深深一躬，毕恭毕敬，又侧身让开，好教侍女跟上自家小姐。吉尔达对他最后那篇堂而皇之的长篇大论，只低低地、含糊地应了一句：「谢谢您，勋爵大人。」随即快步登上踏级，终于在上层楼面走出了他的视野。

有一小会儿，他一动不动地立着，听她在头顶上的脚步声。此刻，他那张瘦削而棱角分明的脸上，只余一片满足。成功——完满的、十足的成功——终于到手了！半月不到之前，他还不过是个失意的流浪客：无家，无亲，无前程；被他所爱的女人鄙弃，被得胜的情敌轻蔑，被出生的土地放逐。

而今天，他的情敌死了——在每一个正派人眼里，那不过是一件只配鄙夷、连怜悯都不值的东西；吉尔达呢，像一枚熟透甘美的果子，眼看就要落进他的怀抱。而且，他的一只脚已经稳稳踏上了御阶。

### V / 五

===Original===
And now the midday hour had gone by, and silence, absolute, reigned in and around the molen. Stoutenburg had spend some time talking to the captain in command of his guard, had himself seen to it that the men were well concealed in the rear of the molen. The horses had been sent down to the camp so as to preclude any possibility of an alarm being given before the apportioned time. Two men were stationed on the platform to keep a lookout upon the distance, where anon the Stadtholder and his troop would appear.

Indeed, everything was ordained and arranged with perfect precision in anticipation of the great coup which was destined to deliver Maurice of Nassau into the hands of his enemy. Everything!—provided that blind informer who lay dead in the banqueting hall of the stately house at Amersfoort had not lied from first to last.

But even if he had lied, even if the Stadtholder had not planned this expedition, or, having planned it, had abandoned it or given up the thought of leading it in person—even so, Stoutenburg was prepared to be satisfied. Already his busy brain was full of plans, which he would put into execution if the present one did not yield him the supreme prize. Gilda was his now, whatever happened. Gilda, and her wealth, and the influence of the Burgomaster Beresteyn, henceforth irrevocably tied to the chariot wheel of his son-in-law. A vista of riches, of honours, of power, was stretched out before the longing gaze of this restless and ambitious self-seeker.

For the nonce, he could afford to wait, even though the hours crept by leaden-footed, and the lookout men up on the platform had nothing as yet to report. The soldiers outside, wrapped up in horse-blankets, squatted against the walls of the dilapidated building, trying to get shelter from the cutting north wind. They had their provisions for the day requisitioned at Barneveld; but these they soon consumed for want of something better to do. The cold was bitter, and anon an icy drizzle began to fall.
===Chinese===
如今正午已过，磨坊内外，一片绝对的寂静。斯托滕贝格花了些时辰同统带卫队的上尉交谈，又亲自查看，务必使人马妥帖地藏进磨坊后身。马匹都已打发下山进了营盘，免得在指定时刻之前有半点走漏风声的可能。平台上派了两名士兵，瞭望远方——执政和他的人马，转眼就要从那面出现了。

的确，为了那桩注定要把拿骚的莫里斯送进仇敌之手的大手笔，一切都已部署安排得分毫不差。万无一失！——只除非那个躺在阿默斯福特华宅宴会厅里的盲眼告密者，从头到尾句句是谎。

但纵然他撒了谎，纵然执政根本不曾筹划这趟出征，或者筹划之后又作罢、又打消了亲征的念头——即便如此，斯托滕贝格也自有满足的本钱。他那颗闲不住的脑袋早已塞满了种种方略，眼前这一桩若捞不到那最高的彩头，他便换一桩施行。吉尔达如今是他的人了，无论出什么变故。吉尔达，连同她的财富，连同市长贝雷斯坦的势力，从此不可逆转地拴在他这东床婿的战车轮上。财富、荣衔、权势的远景，在这躁动而野心勃勃的自私自利者渴望的凝望面前，一一铺展开来。

眼下，他等得起，哪怕时辰迈着铅脚步子磨蹭，哪怕平台上瞭望的士兵至今无可禀报。屋外的士兵裹着马毯，蹲靠在破楼的墙根下，想躲一躲砭骨的北风。他们当日的口粮是在巴讷费尔德征来的；可闲得发慌，不多时便吃了个精光。天冷得厉害，转眼又落起冰冷的蒙蒙细雨来。

### VI / 六

===Original===
Stoutenburg, inside the millhouse, had started on that restless pacing up and down which was so characteristic of him. He had ordered the best of the provisions to be taken up to the jongejuffrouw and her maid. He himself had eaten and had drunk, and now he had nothing to do but wait. And think. Anon he got tired of both, and when he heard the women moving about overhead, he suddenly paused in his fretful wanderings, pondered for a moment or two, and then went resolutely up the stairs.

Gilda was sitting on a pile of sacking; her hands lay idly in her lap. With a curt word of command, Stoutenburg ordered the waiting woman to go below.

Then he approached Gilda, and half-kneeling, half-reclining by her side, he tried to take her hand. But she evaded him, hid her hands underneath her cloak. This apparently vastly amused his lordship, for he laughed good-humouredly, and said, with an ardent look of passionate admiration:
===Chinese===
磨坊屋里，斯托滕贝格开始了那种他特有的、心神不定的一来一去的踱步。他吩咐把最好的吃食给小姐和她的侍女送上楼去。他自己吃过也喝过了，如今除了等待，再无事可做。还有思索。不多时，两样他都腻了；听见女人们在头顶上走动，他忽然在焦躁的徘徊中收住脚，思忖了一两分钟，随即毅然上楼。

吉尔达正坐在一堆麻袋上，双手懒懒地搁在膝头。斯托滕贝格一声简短的命令，打发侍女到楼下去。

随后他挨近吉尔达，半跪半倚在她身旁，想去握她的手。她却避开了，把手藏进斗篷底下。这显然让勋爵大人觉着大为有趣，他兴致很好地笑起来，带着热烈倾慕的神色说道：

===Original===
“That is where you are so desirable, *myn engel*. Never twice the same. Awhile ago you seemed as yielding as a dove; now once more I see the young vixen peeping at me through those wonderful blue eyes. Well!” he added with a sigh of contentment, “I will not complain. Life by your side, *myn geliefde*, will never be dull. The zest of taming a beautiful shrew must ever be a manly sport.”

Then, as she made no sign either of defiance or comprehension, but sat with eyes strained and neck craned forward, almost as if she were listening, he raised himself and sat down upon the sacking close beside her. She puzzled him now, as she always did; and that puzzlement added zest to his wooing.

“I was waxing so dejected down below,” he said, and leaned forward, his lips almost touching the hood that kept her ears concealed. “Little did I guess that so much delight lay ready to my hand. Time is a hard taskmaster to me just now, and I have not the leisure to make as ardent love to you as I would wish. But I have the time to gratify a fancy, and this I will do. My fancy is to have three kisses from your sweet lips on mine. Three, and no more, and on the lips, *myn schat*.”
===Chinese===
「这正是你叫人心醉的地方，我的天使。从来没有两回是同一个样子的。方才你还温顺得像只鸽子；这会儿我又看见那只小雌狐，打那双奇妙的蓝眼睛里窥伺我了。好啊！」他心满意足地叹了一声，又接着说，「我不抱怨。在你身边过日子，我的爱人（*myn geliefde*），永远不会乏味。驯服一头美丽的悍妇，其乐无穷，永远是男子汉的运动。」

见她既无倔抗之色，也无理会之意，只管绷紧了眼睛、伸着脖颈坐在那儿，几乎像在侧耳倾听，他便直起身子，紧挨着她在麻袋上坐了下来。此刻她又叫他摸不透了，一如往常；而这层摸不透，给他这场求欢更添了几分滋味。

「我在楼下正闷得发慌呢，」他说着探身向前，嘴唇几乎碰到那顶掩着她双耳的兜帽。「万万想不到，这么多乐趣竟唾手可得。眼下光阴待我苛刻得很，我没有闲工夫照我的心愿，对你大献那种炽热的殷勤。可我还有工夫遂一个心愿，这一件我一定要遂。我的心愿，是要你那甜蜜的嘴唇，在我唇上印三个吻。三个，多一个不要，要亲在唇上，我的宝贝。」

===Original===
In an instant his arms were round her. But equally suddenly she had evaded him. She jumped up and ran, as swift as a hare, to the farther end of the loft, where she remained ensconced behind a transverse beam, her arms round it for support, her face, white and set, only vaguely discernible in the gloom.

The dim afternoon light which came but shyly peeping in through two small windows high up in the walls, failed to reach this angle of the loft where Gilda had found shelter. With this dim background behind her, she appeared like some elusive spectre, an apparition, without form or substance, her face and hands alone visible.

When she escaped him, Stoutenburg had cursed, as was his wont, then struggled to his feet and tried to carry off the situation with an affected laugh. But somehow the girls’ face, there in the semidarkness, gave him an unpleasant, eerie sensation. He did not follow her, but paused in the centre of the loft, laughter dying upon his lips.
===Chinese===
转瞬间，他的双臂已经箍住她。可她也同样突然地挣脱了。她跳起来，像野兔一般迅捷，直奔阁楼最远的一头，随即隐在一根横梁后面不动了，双臂抱着梁柱撑住身子，那张绷紧发白的脸，在幽暗里只能约略辨出轮廓。

午后幽暗的天光怯生生地从墙上高处两扇小窗里窥进来，照不到吉尔达藏身的这个角落。背后衬着这一片昏昧，她像一个飘忽的幽灵，一个幻影，无形无质，只有脸和手隐约可见。

她挣脱的那一瞬，斯托滕贝格照他的老脾气咒骂了一声，随即撑起身子，想装出一声笑把这场面应付过去。可不知怎么，姑娘的脸在那半明半暗里，给了他一种不快的、鬼气森森的感觉。他没有追过去，只在阁楼中央收住脚，笑意在他唇上渐渐敛尽。

===Original===
“Am I to remind you again, you little termagant,” he said, with a great show of bluster, “that Jan is still at Amersfoort, and that I may yet send a runner to him if I have a mind, ordering that by nightfall that accursed city be ablaze?”

He was looking straight at her while he spoke. And she returned his glance, but gave him no reply. Just for the space of a few seconds an extraordinary stillness appeared to have descended upon the molen. Up here, in the loft, nothing stirred, nothing was heard above that silence save the patter of the rain upon the roof overhead against the tiny window panes. For a few seconds, whilst Stoutenburg stood like a beast of prey about to spring, and Gilda, still and silent, like a bird on the alert.

And suddenly, even as he gazed, the man’s expression slowly underwent a change. First the arrogance died out of it, the forced irony. Every line became set, then rigid, and more and more ashen in hue, until the whole face appeared like a death-mask, colourless and transparent as wax, the jaw dropping, the lips parted as for a cry that would not come. And the sunken eyes opened wider and wider, and wider still as they gazed, not on Gilda any longer, but into the darkness behind her, whilst the whole aspect of the man was like a living statute of horror and of a nameless fear.

Then suddenly, right through the silence and above the weird patter of the rain, there rang a sound which roused the very echoes that lay dormant among the ancient rafters. So strange a sound was it that when it reached his ear, Stoutenburg lost his balance and swayed on his feet like a drunken man; so strange that Gilda, her nerves giving way for the first time under the terrible strain which she had undergone, buried her face against her arms, whilst a loud sob broke from her throat. Yet the sound in itself was neither a terrifying nor a tragic one. It was just the sound of a prolonged and loud peal of laughter.
===Chinese===
「还要我再提醒你一次么，你这小泼妇，」他摆出一副大呼小叫的架势说道，「扬还在阿默斯福特，只要我高兴，随时可以再派一名信使去传令：天黑之前，叫那座可诅咒的城烧成一片火海？」

说话时，他直盯着她。她也迎住他的目光，却不答一言。就有那么几秒钟，一种异乎寻常的静谧仿佛罩临了整座磨坊。在这上面的阁楼里，万籁不动，除了雨点敲打头顶屋顶、敲打小窗玻璃的声响，那片寂静之上再听不见别的。有几秒钟，斯托滕贝格立在那儿，像一头蓄势欲扑的猛兽；吉尔达则静止无声，像一只警觉的小鸟。

突然间，就在他凝望之际，这张脸慢慢变了。先是傲气消了下去，强作的讥诮也消了下去。每一条纹路先凝住，继而僵住，颜色越来越灰白，直到整张脸活像一副死面模，蜡也似的无色而近乎透明，下颌垂落，双唇张开，像要喊一声却喊不出来。那双凹陷的眼睛越睁越大，越睁越大，望着已不是吉尔达，而是她身后的黑暗；这人的整个神态，活像一尊恐怖与无名惊惧铸成的活雕像。

接着陡然间，就在这片寂静里，压过雨点古怪的敲打，响起了一个声音，惊醒了沉睡在古老椽木间的回声。这声音怪到这般地步：一入耳，斯托滕贝格便立足不稳，像个醉汉似的摇晃起来；怪到这般地步，吉尔达——她在这场可怕的煎熬下，神经头一回支撑不住——把脸埋进双臂，喉咙里迸出一声响亮的呜咽。然而这声音本身，既不可怕，也不悲惨。那不过是一阵悠长响亮的大笑。

===Original===
“By my halidame!” a merry voice swore lustily. “But meseems that your lordship had no thought of seeing me here!”

Just for a few seconds, superstitious fear held the miscreant gripped by the throat. A few seconds? To him, to Gilda, they seemed an eternity. Then a hoarse whisper escaped him.

“Spectre or demon, which are you?”

“Both, you devil!” the mocking voice gave reply. “And I would send you down to hell and shoot you like a dog where you stand, but for the noise which would bring your men about mine ears.”
===Chinese===
「我的圣徒在上！」一个欢快的声音纵声起誓。「依我看，勋爵大人可没想到会在这儿见到我吧！」

就有那么几秒钟，迷信的恐惧扼住了这恶徒的喉咙。几秒钟？对他，对吉尔达，那都像是永恒。随后，一声嘶哑的耳语从他唇间漏了出来。

「你是鬼魂，还是魔鬼？」

「都是，你这魔鬼！」那嘲弄的声音答道。「要不是怕枪声把你的兵卒引到我跟前，我这就送你下地狱，叫你像条狗一样毙在你站的地方。」

===Original===
“To hell yourself, you infamous *plepshurk*!” Stoutenburg cried, strove to shake off with a mighty effort the superstitious dread that made a weakling of him. He fumbled for his sword, succeeded in drawing it from its scabbard, and cursed himself for being without a pistol in his belt.

“Where you came from, I know not,” he went on in a husky whisper. “But be you wraith or demon, you—”

He seemed to speak involuntarily, as if sheer terror was forcing the words through his bloodless lips. Suddenly he uttered a hoarse cry:

“*A moi!* Somebody there! *A moi!*”
===Chinese===
「你自己下地狱去吧，你这无耻的蠢货（*plepshurk*）！」斯托滕贝格喝道，奋力想抖落那份把他变成懦夫的迷信恐惧。他摸索佩剑，总算把它从鞘里拔了出来，一面暗骂自己腰带上没带手枪。

「你从哪儿来，我不知道，」他哑着嗓子低声往下说。「可不管你是亡灵还是魔鬼，你——」

他说得像是身不由己，仿佛纯粹的恐怖正把那些字句从他毫无血色的唇间往外挤。陡然间，他发出一声嘶哑的喊叫：

「来人啊！（*A moi*!）有人吗！来人啊！」

===Original===
But the walls of the old molen were thick, and his voice, spent and still half-choked with the horror of that spectral apparition, refused him effective service. It failed to carry far enough. The tiny windows were impracticable; the soldiers were outside at the rear of the building, out of earshot; and down below there was only the old waiting woman.

“That *smeerlap*!” he cried, half to himself. “Either a wraith or blind. In either case—”

And, sword in hand, he rushed upon his mocking enemy. A blind man! Bah! What had he to fear? The rogue had in truth thrust Gilda behind him. He stood there, with one of those short English daggers in his hand, which had of late put the fine Toledo blades to shame. But a blind man, for all that! How he had escaped out of Amersfoort, and by whose connivance Stoutenburg had not time to think. But the man was blind. Every phase of last evening’s interview with him—the vacant eyes, the awkward movements—stood out clearly before his lordship’s mental vision, and testified to that one fact; the man was blind and helpless.

Crouching like a feline creature upon his haunches, Stoutenburg was ready for a spring. His every movement became lithe and silent as that of a snake. He had marked out to himself just how and where he would strike. He only waited until those eyes—those awful eyes—ceased to look on him. But their glance never wavered. They followed his ever step. They mocked and derided and threatened withal! By Satan and all his hordes! those stricken orbs could see!
===Chinese===
可老磨坊的墙壁厚实，他的嗓音已然力竭，又还被那幽灵现身的恐怖噎住了半截，不肯替他出力。声音传不了那么远。小窗派不上用场；士兵们都在屋外后身，听不见；楼下面只有一个老侍女。

「那个龌龊货（*smeerlap*）！」他半是自语地叫道。「不是个亡灵，就是个瞎子。不管哪一样——」

于是他提剑在手，直扑那个嘲弄他的仇敌。一个瞎子！呸！他有什么可怕的？那恶棍果真把吉尔达推到了自己身后。他站在那儿，手里握着一柄英式短匕——近来这种匕首已把精良的托莱多名剑比了下去。可说到底，是个瞎子！他是怎么逃出阿默斯福特的，又得了谁的默许纵容，斯托滕贝格无暇细想。可这人是个瞎子。昨晚会见他时的每一幕——那双空洞的眼睛，那笨拙的举动——都清清楚楚地立在勋爵大人心头，为这一个事实作证：这人是个瞎子，孤立无援。

斯托滕贝格像一头猫科猛兽，踞蹲蓄势，随时可以扑出。他的一举一动，都变得像蛇一般柔韧无声。该怎样出手、往哪里下手，他心里已盘算停当。他只等那双眼睛——那双可怖的眼睛——移开他不看的当口。可那目光从不游移。他挪一步，它跟一步。它嘲弄着、讥笑着，还带着威吓！凭撒旦和他麾下群魔起誓！那双遭殃的眼珠看得见！

===Original===
At what precise moment that conviction entered Stoutenburg’s tortured brain, he could not himself have told you. But suddenly it was there. And in an instant his nerve completely forsook him. An icy sweat broke all over his body. His head swam, his knees gave way under him, the sword dropped out of his nerveless hand. Then, with a quick hoarse cry, he turned to flee. His foot was on the top step of the ladder which led to the room below. A prolonged, mocking laugh behind him seemed to lend him wings. But freedom—aye, and more!—beckoned from below. There was only an old woman there, and his soldiers were outside. Ye gods! He was a fool to fear!

He flew down the few steps, nearly fell headlong in the act, for his nerves were playing him an unpleasant trick, and the afternoon light was growing dim. At first, when he reached the place below, he saw nothing. Nothing save the welcome door, straight before him which led straight to freedom from this paralysing obsession. With one bound he had covered half the intervening space, when suddenly he paused, and an awful curse rose to his lips. There, in the recess of the doorway, two men were squatting on their heels, intent upon a game of hazard. One of these men was long and lean, the other round as a curled-up hedgehog. They did no more than glance over their shoulder when His Magnificence the Lord of Stoutenburg came staggering down the steps.

“Five and four,” the lean vagabond was saying. “How many does that make?”

“Eight, you loon!” the other replied. “My turn now.”
===Chinese===
这个确信究竟在哪一瞬钻进斯托滕贝格备受煎熬的脑袋，他自己也说不上来。可它突然就在那儿了。一刹那间，他的胆气散了个干净。一身冰冷的汗水涔涔而下。他头晕目眩，双膝发软，长剑从失了知觉的手里掉落在地。随后，他一声急促嘶哑的喊叫，转身便逃。他一脚已踏上通向楼下房间的那道梯子最上一级。身后一阵拖长的嘲弄大笑，仿佛给他插上了翅膀。而下面——自由，是啊，何止自由！——正向他招手。下面只有一个老太婆，他的士兵就在屋外。众神哪！他害怕，真是傻瓜！

他飞也似的奔下没几级的梯级，差点一头栽倒——他的神经正跟他捣着不客气的乱，午后的天光也渐渐暗了。刚到楼下，起先他什么也没看见。什么也没有，只有那扇受欢迎的大门，端端正正在他面前，直通向自由，逃离这场叫人神智瘫痪的魔障。他一个箭步已蹿过半程的距离，忽地收住脚，一声可怕的咒骂涌上唇边。门洞的凹处，两个人正蹲踞在地，专心致志掷一场骰子赌戏。这两人一个瘦长，一个圆滚滚的像只蜷起的刺猬。大人阁下斯托滕贝格勋爵踉踉跄跄奔下梯级时，他们至多不过回头瞥了一眼。

「五和四，」瘦长个儿的流浪汉正说着。「一共是几？」

「八个，你这呆子！」另一个答道。「该我了。」

===Original===
They continued their game, regardless of his lordship who stood there rooted to the spot, trembling in every limb, his body covered with sweat, feeling like an animal that sees a trap slowly closing in upon him.

The situation was indeed one to send a man out of his senses. Stoutenburg, for one brief instant, felt that he was going mad. He looked from the door to the steps, and back again to the door, marvelling which way lay his one chance of escape. If he shouted, would he be heard? Could his men get to him before those two ruffians fell to and murdered him? Dared he make a dash for the door? Or—It was unthinkable that he—Stoutenburg—should be standing here, at the mercy of three villains, utterly powerless, when outside, not fifty paces away, the other side of those walls, fifty men at arms were there, set to guard his person.

And suddenly fear fell away from him. The trembling of his limbs ceased, his vision became clear, his mind alert. Even around his quaking lips there came the ghost of a smile.

His senses, keyed up by the imminence of his danger, had seized upon a sound which came from outside, faint as yet, but very obviously drawing nearer. In the semidarkness and with his head buzzing and his nerves tingling, he could not distinguish either the quality of the sound nor yet the exact direction whence it came. But whatever it was—even if it was not all that he hoped—the sound was bound to set his soldiers on the alert; and if he could only temporize with those ruffians for a minute or two, the very next would see the captain of his guard rushing in to report what was happening: That Stadtholder sighted, the signal given, Nicolaes Beresteyn coming swiftly to the rescue.
===Chinese===
他们自顾自继续赌下去，全不理会勋爵大人——他钉在原地，四肢发颤，浑身汗透，像一头眼看着陷阱慢慢合拢过来的困兽。

这局面，实在能把人逼疯。有一瞬间，斯托滕贝格真觉得自己要疯了。他看看门，又看看梯级，再回头看看门，拿不准哪一条才是他唯一的生路。他若喊叫，能有人听见么？他那两个恶棍动手杀他之前，他的士兵来得及赶到么？他敢不敢夺门硬冲？要不——不可想象，他——斯托滕贝格——竟会站在这里，任三个恶徒摆布，全然无能为力，而墙外不出五十步远的地方，明明有五十名执兵之士，专为护卫他的人身而设。

陡然间，恐惧从他身上褪了下去。四肢的颤抖止了，眼目清明，心神警醒。就连他哆嗦的唇边，也浮起了一丝笑影。

他的感官被迫在眉睫的危险绷到了极处，捕捉到了一个从外面传来的声音——尚且微弱，却分明在逼近。半明半暗里，他脑中嗡嗡作响，神经灼灼刺痛，既辨不出那声音的性质，也辨不出它确切来自哪个方向。可不管它是什么——纵然不是他全然盼望的那一桩——这声音必会让他的士兵警觉起来；他只消同那两个恶徒周旋得一两分钟，下一刻便会看见卫队统领飞奔进来禀报军情：执政已然在望，信号已经发出，尼古拉斯·贝雷斯坦正兼程来援。

===Original===
Therefore, in the face of his own imminent peril, the Lord of Stoutenburg no longer felt afraid, only tensely vitally expectant. The two caitiffs, on the other hand appeared to have heard nothing. At any rate, they went on with their game, and the flute-like, high-pitched tones of the fat loon alternated with the deep base of his companion:

“Three and two make five!”

“No, four, you varlet!”

“Six!”

“Blank, by Beelzebub! My luck is dead out today.”
===Chinese===
因此，纵然大祸悬在头顶，斯托滕贝格勋爵也不再害怕，只是紧张地、生机勃勃地期待着。那两个卑劣之徒呢，看上去却什么也没听见。至少，他们照赌不误，胖子那笛子似的高尖嗓音，同他同伴的低沉喉音此起彼伏：

「三加二，是五！」

「不，是四，你这无赖！」

「六！」

「空的，凭别西卜（Beelzebub）起誓！我今天手气糟透啦。」

===Original===
And the sound drew nearer. There was no mistaking it. Men running. The clatter of arms. Horses, too. A pawing, and a champing, and a general hubbub, which those two ruffians could not fail to hear. Nor did any sound come down from the loft. Yet Gilda was there with the miserable *plepshurk* who, whatever else happened, would inevitably stand before her now as an informer and a cheat. This, at any rate, was a fact. The man had betrayed his master in order to save his miserable life, and the burgomaster had connived at his escape through an access of doltish weakness. But the fact remained. The Stadtholder was approaching. The next few minutes—seconds, perhaps—would see the final triumphant issue of this terrible adventure.

Stoutenburg, like a feline at bay, waited.

Then, all at once, a musket shot rang through the air, then another, and yet another; and all at once the whole air around was alive with sounds. The clang of arms; the lusty battle cries. Men out there had come to grips. In the drenching rain they were at one another’s throats.
===Chinese===
声音更近了。错不了。人在奔跑。兵甲铿锵。还有马匹。刨蹄声、嚼衔声，乱成一片的嘈杂，那两个恶徒不可能听不见。阁楼上也没有一点声响传下来。然而吉尔达正在那上头，同那个可怜的蠢货在一起——无论往后还出什么事，此人从今在她面前，注定就是个告密者和骗子了。至少，这是桩事实。这人为了保住自己那条可怜的性命，出卖了他的主子；市长则因为一阵愚蠢的心软，默许了他的逃脱。可事实终归是事实。执政正在逼近。再过几分钟——也许几秒钟——这场可怕的冒险，便将见出最后凯旋的结局。

斯托滕贝格像一头陷于绝境的猫，等着。

随后，陡然间一声火枪划破长空，接着又是一声，再一声；刹那间，四下里的空气都活了过来，满是声响。兵器的铿锵；豪壮的呐喊。外头的人已经短兵相接。滂沱大雨里，他们正扼着彼此的咽喉厮拼。

===Original===
The two caitiffs quietly put aside their dice and rose to their feet. They stood with their backs to the door, their eyes fixed upon his lordship.

“Stand aside, you dolts!” Stoutenburg cried aloud; for he thought that he read murder in those two pairs of eyes, and he had need of all his nerves to assure himself that all was well, that, though his captain had not come to him for a reason which no doubt was sound, his soldiers were at grips with the Stadtholder’s vanguard, and Nicolaes was already halfway up the slope.

But he, Stoutenburg, was unarmed, and could not push past those two assassins who were guarding the door. He bethought himself of his sword, which lay on the floor of the loft. He turned with a sudden impulse to get hold of it at all costs, and was met at the very foot of the steps by the man who had baffled him at every turn.

Diogenes, sword in hand, did not even pause to look on his impotent enemy. With one spring, he was across the floor and out by the door, which one of the ruffians immediately closed behind him.
===Chinese===
两个卑劣之徒悄悄搁下骰子，站起身来。他们背朝着大门站定，眼睛盯住了勋爵大人。

「让开，你们这两个呆子！」斯托滕贝格大声喝道；因为他觉得从那两双眼睛里读出的是杀机，而他需要拿出全部的神经来稳住自己，让自己相信一切无恙：虽然他的统领没有进来禀报——那缘由想必是充分的——他的士兵已同执政的前锋交上了手，尼古拉斯也已冲上半坡了。

可他，斯托滕贝格，手无寸铁，闯不过那两个把门的刺客。他想起自己的剑还撂在阁楼的地板上。他心念一动，转身不顾一切也要去取剑，却在梯级脚下，迎面撞上了那个处处叫他棋输一着的人。

第欧根尼提剑在手，甚至不曾停下看他那束手无策的仇敌一眼。他一个纵跃便穿过屋面，出门而去，一个恶徒随即把门在他身后关上了。

===Original===
It had all happened swifter even than thought. Stoutenburg, trapped, helpless, more bewildered in truth than terrified, still believed in a happy issue to his present desperate position. The thought came to him that he might purchase his safety from those potential murderers.

“Ten thousand guilders,” he called out wildly, “if you will let me pass!”

But the fat *runnion* merely turned to the lean one, and the look of understanding which passed between them sent an icy shudder down his lordship’s spine. He knew that from these two he could expect no mercy. A hoarse cry of horror escaped his lips as he saw that each held a dagger in one hand.

Then began that awful chase when man becomes a hunted beast—that grim game of hide-and-seek, with the last issue never once in doubt. The Lord of Stoutenburg trapped between these narrow walls, ran round and round like a mouse in a cage; now seeking refuge behind a girder, now leaping over an intervening obstacle, now crouching, panting and bathed in sweat, under cover of the gloom. And no one spoke; no one called. Neither the hunted nor the hunters. It seemed as if a conspiracy of silence existed between them; or else that the nearness of death had put a seal on all their lips.
===Chinese===
这一切来得比思想还快。斯托滕贝格落了网，无路可走，说实话与其说是吓破了胆，不如说是懵了神智，却还相信他眼下这走投无路的处境会有一个吉利的收场。他忽然想到，兴许可以从那两个潜在的杀人凶徒手里，买得一条生路。

「一万盾，」他狂呼道，「放我过去，钱就是你们的！」

可那胖子无赖（*runnion*）只朝瘦子转过脸去，两人之间交换的那一个会意的眼色，叫一股冰冷的寒颤顺着勋爵大人的脊梁直灌下去。他知道，从这两人身上，他休想指望半分怜悯。待看见两人各自一手都握着一柄匕首，一声嘶哑的惊骇喊叫从他唇间迸了出来。

于是开始了那场可怕的追逐——人沦为被猎野兽的那场追逐——那场阴森的捉迷藏，结局如何，自始至终不曾有过一丝悬念。斯托滕贝格勋爵被困在这几面窄墙之间，像笼中老鼠，一圈又一圈地乱转；一时躲到一根大梁后头，一时跃过挡道的障碍，一时又伏进昏暗的掩蔽里，喘息着，汗透重衣。没有人说话；没有人喊叫。被追的和追人的都不出声。仿佛他们之间存在着一桩沉默的共谋；又或者，是死亡的临近，给所有这些嘴唇都贴上了封条。

===Original===
Out there the clang of battle appeared more remote. Nothing seemed to occur in the immediate approach of the molen. It all came from afar, resounding across the Veluwe, above the patter of the rain and the soughing of the wind, through the rafters of the old mill. Drumming and thumping, the angle of armour, the clang of pike and lance, of metal; the loud report of musket shot, the strident grating of chains and wheels. But all far away, not here. Not outside the molen, but down there in the gorge, where Nicolaes had been encamped. My heavens, what did it mean?

Already the trapped creature was getting exhausted. Once or twice he had come down on his knees. His eyes were growing dim. His breath came and went with a wheezing sound from his breast. It was not just two murderous brigands who were pursuing him, but Nemesis herself, with sword of retribution drawn, in her hand an hourglass, the sands of which were running low.

All at once the miscreant found himself at the foot of the steps, and, blindly stumbling, he ran up to the loft—instinctively, without set purpose save that of warding off, if only for a minute, the inevitable end.
===Chinese===
外面，厮杀的铿锵听上去更远了。磨坊左近似乎毫无动静。一切都从远处传来，越过费吕韦的原野，压过雨点的敲打与风的呜咽，穿进老磨坊的椽木。鼓点般的闷响，铠甲棱角的磕碰，长枪长戟与钢铁的铿锵；火枪轰然的炸响，链条与车轮刺耳的磨轧。可全都在远处，不在这儿。不在磨坊外面，而在下面那道峡沟里，尼古拉斯扎营的地方。我的天，这是什么意思？

这落入陷阱的可怜虫已经力竭了。有一两回，他跪倒在地。他的眼睛渐渐发花。气息在他胸口进出，带着嘶嘶的喘鸣。追逐他的，已不只是两个杀人越货的强盗，而是复仇女神涅墨西斯（Nemesis）本人：惩罪之剑已然出鞘，另一只手里擎着沙漏，漏中的沙已所剩无几。

陡然间，这恶徒发觉自己已到了梯级脚下，便跌跌撞撞、盲盲目目地奔上阁楼去——全凭本能，谈不上什么盘算，只求把那不可避免的结局，哪怕多挡开一分钟。

### VII / 七

===Original===
Gilda was standing at the top of the steps with neck craned forward, her hands held tightly to her breast, her whole attitude one of nameless horror. She had been listening to the multifarious sounds which came from outside, and the natural, womanly fear for the safety of her beloved had been her one dominant emotion.

She had heard nothing else for a time, until suddenly she caught one or two stray sounds of that grim and furtive fight for life which was going on down below. She had reached the top of the steps, and tried to peer through the gloom to ascertain whose were those stealthy, swift footfalls so like those of a hunted beast, and whose the heavy, lumbering tread that spoke of stern and unwavering pursuit. At first she could see nothing, and the very silence which lay like a pall upon the grim scene below struck her with a sense of paralysing dread.

Then she caught sight first of one figure, then of another, as they crossed her line of vision. She could distinguish nothing very clearly—just those slowly moving figures—and for a moment or two felt herself unable to move. Then she heard the laboured breathing of a man, a groan as of a soul tortured with fear, and the next instant the Lord of Stoutenburg appeared, stumbling up the narrow steps.
===Chinese===
吉尔达立在梯级顶端，脖颈前伸，双手紧紧按在胸口，整个姿态透着一种无以名状的恐怖。她一直在听外面传来的种种声响，而为心上人安危担着的那份女人天生的忧惧，便是她心头压倒一切的情绪。

有一阵子，她别的什么也没听见，直到蓦地捕捉到一两声零落的动静——楼下正进行着那场阴森而鬼祟的求生之斗。她奔到梯级顶端，竭力望穿幽暗，想辨明那轻悄迅疾、活像被猎野兽的脚步是谁的，那沉重拖沓、透着严酷而不歇追逼的踏声又是谁的。起先她什么也看不见，而笼罩在楼下可怖场景上的那片死寂，像一床尸衣，给了她一种令人瘫软的惊惧。

随后，她先后望见了两条人影，从她的视线上横过。她辨不真切——只有那几条缓缓移动的身影——有一两刻，她觉得自己动弹不得。接着，她听见一个男人吃力的喘息，一声仿佛灵魂受着恐惧煎熬的呻吟；下一瞬，斯托滕贝格勋爵便跌跌撞撞地出现在那道窄窄的楼梯上。

===Original===
At sight of her he fell like an inert thing with a husky cry at her feet. His arms encircled her knees; his head fell against her gown.

“Gilda, save me!” he whispered hoarsely. “For the love of Heaven! They’ll murder me! Save me, for pity’s sake! Gilda!”

He sobbed and cried like a child, abject in his terror, loathsome in his craven cowardice. Gilda could not stir. He held her with his arms as in a vise. She would have given worlds for the physical strength to wrench her gown out of his clutch, to flee from the hated sight of him who had planned to do her beloved such an irreparable injury. Oh, she hated him! She hated him worse, perhaps, than she had ever done before, now that he clung like a miserable dastard to her for mercy.
===Chinese===
一看见她，他便像一件死物，带着一声嘶哑的叫喊倒在她脚下。他的双臂箍住她的双膝；他的头抵在她的裙裾上。

「吉尔达，救救我！」他嘶哑地低语。「看在上天的分上！他们要杀我！发发慈悲，救救我！吉尔达！」

他像个孩子似的又哭又嚎，恐怖中卑劣至极，懦怯中可憎至极。吉尔达动弹不得。他用双臂像老虎钳一样箍住了她。只要有力气把裙裾从他抓握里挣脱出来，逃离这可恨的身影——那个蓄谋要给她心上人造成无可挽回的伤害的人——她拿整个世界来换也甘心。啊，她恨他！此刻他像条可怜的胆小鬼似的攀着她乞怜，她对他的恨，也许比以往任何时候都更深。

===Original===
“Leave the poltroon to us, mejuffrouw,” a gentle, flute-like tone broke in on the miscreant’s ravings.

“Now then, take your punishment like a man!” a gruff voice added sternly.

And two familiar faces emerged out of the gloom, immediately below where Gilda was standing, imprisoned by those cringing arms. The man, in truth, had not even the primeval pluck of a savage. He was beaten, and he knew it. What had happened out there on the Veluwe, how completely he had been tricked by the Englishman he did not know as yet. But he was afraid to die, and shrank neither from humiliation nor contempt in order to save his own worthless life from the wreck of all his ambitions.
===Chinese===
「把这胆小鬼交给我们吧，小姐，」一个温和的、笛子般的嗓音，打断了这恶徒的语无伦次。

「好了，拿出男子汉的样子来领受你的惩罚吧！」一个粗嘎的声音凛然补上一句。

两张熟悉的脸从幽暗里浮现出来，就在吉尔达立处下面一点——她还被那双委顿的手臂囚着。这个人啊，说实话，连野蛮人那点原始的血性都没有。他败了，他自己心里清楚。费吕韦原野上出了什么事，那个英国人把他骗得多么彻底，他一时还不知就里。可他怕死，为了从他那全部野心的残骸里救出自己这条不值钱的性命，屈辱也好、鄙夷也好，他一概都受得住。

===Original===
At the sound of those two voices, which in truth were like a death-knell in his ears, he jumped to his feet; but he did not loosen his hold on Gilda. Swift as thought he had found refuge behind her, and held her by the arms in front of him like a shield.

Historians have always spoken of the Lord of Stoutenburg as extraordinarily nimble in mind and body. That nimbleness in truth, stood him in good stead now; or whilst Socrates and Pythagoras, clumsy in their movements, lumbering and hampered by their respect for the person of the jongejuffrouw, reached the loft, and then for one instant hesitated how best to proceed in their grim task without offending the ears and eyes of the great lady, Stoutenburg had with one bound slipped from behind her down the steps and was across the floor of the molen and out the door before the two worthies had had time to utter the comprehensive curse which, at this unexpected manoeuvre on the part of their quarry, had risen to their lips.

“We had promised Diogenes not to allow the blackguard to escape!” Pythagoras exclaimed ruefully.

And both started in hot pursuit, whilst Gilda, seeking shelter in a dark angle of the loft, fell, sobbing with excitement and only half-conscious, upon a pile of sacking.
===Chinese===
一听见那两个嗓音——它们于他委实如丧钟灌耳——他霍地跳了起来；却不曾放松对吉尔达的钳制。他疾如念头一闪，已躲到她身后，抓着她的双臂挡在自己身前，当作一面盾牌。

史家们素来称斯托滕贝格勋爵身心两健、机敏过人。这份机敏，此刻倒真帮了他的大忙：当苏格拉底（Socrates）和毕达哥拉斯（Pythagoras）两人——举动笨拙，拖拖沓沓，又碍着对小姐之身的敬重——好容易上了阁楼，一时还踌躇着这桩狠差事该怎样办得最妥，方不致污了这位贵妇人的耳目，就趁这一瞬，斯托滕贝格已从她身后一个箭步溜下梯级，穿过磨坊地面，出了大门，等那两位好汉来得及开口，那句总揽全局的咒骂——因他们猎物这出乎意料的一手，早已涌上唇边——竟没能出口。

「我们答应过第欧根尼，不能放这恶棍跑掉！」毕达哥拉斯懊恼地叫道。

两人当即拔腿猛追；吉尔达躲进阁楼一角暗处，激动得抽抽噎噎、神志半昏，倒在了一堆麻袋上。

## XVI The Final Issue / XVI 最终结局

### I / 一

===Original===
Pythagoras and Socrates failed to find the trail of the miscreant, who had vanished under cover of the night. We know that Stoutenburg did succeed, in fact, in reaching de Berg’s encampment, half-starved and wearied, but safe. How he did it, no one will ever know. His career of crime had received a mighty check and the marauding expeditions which he undertook subsequently against his own country were of a futile and desultory nature. History ceases to trouble herself about him after that abortive incursion into Gelderland.

How that incursion was frustrated by the gallant Englishman, known to fame as the first Sir Percy Blakeney, but to his intimates as Diogenes, the erstwhile penniless soldier of fortune, we know chiefly through van Aitzema’s *Saken von Staet*. The worthy chronicler enlarges upon the Englishman’s adventure﻿—he always calls him “the Englishman”﻿—from the time when a week and more ago, he took leave of Nicolaes Beresteyn outside Barneveld to that when he reached Amersfoort, just in time to avert a terrible catastrophe.

The author of *Saken v. Staet* tells of the ambuscade on the shores of the Ijssel, “the Englishman’s swim for life through the drifting floes.” On reaching the opposite bank, it seems that he was so spent and more than half frozen, that he lay half unconscious on the bank for awhile. Presently, however, alive to the danger of possible further ambuscades, he restarted on his way, found a deserted hut close by, and crawled in there for shelter. As soon as darkness had set in he started back for Zutphen, there to warn Marquet not to proceed. The whole of the Stadtholder’s plans had obviously been revealed to de Berg by some traitor﻿—whose identity Diogenes then could not fail but guess﻿—and it would have been sheer madness to attempt to cross the Ijssel now at any of the points originally intended.

===Chinese===
毕达哥拉斯（Pythagoras）和苏格拉底（Socrates）没能追到那恶棍的踪迹——他趁着夜色的掩护，消失得无影无踪。我们知道，斯托滕贝格（Stoutenburg）后来倒确实到达了德·贝格（de Berg）的大营，虽然半饥半饱、疲惫不堪，人却安全。他是怎么做到的，永远无人知晓。他的罪恶生涯遭到了一次沉重的挫顿，此后他对自己的祖国发动的几次劫掠远征，都不过是徒劳而零乱的作为。那次进犯海尔德兰（Gelderland）以失败告终之后，历史便再懒得为他费笔墨了。

那次进犯如何被那位英勇的英格兰人挫败——此人名闻天下，是第一代珀西·布莱克尼爵士（Sir Percy Blakeney），而在至交口中则是第欧根尼（Diogenes），昔日那个身无分文的雇佣冒险家——我们所得知的经过，主要出自范·艾特泽马（van Aitzema）的《国家事务》（*Saken von Staet*）。这位可敬的编年史家不吝笔墨，铺陈这位英格兰人的历险——他总是管他叫「那个英格兰人」——从他一星期多以前在巴讷费尔德（Barneveld）城外与尼古拉斯·贝雷斯坦（Nicolaes Beresteyn）分手讲起，一直讲到他抵达阿默斯福特（Amersfoort），恰好来得及遏止一场可怕的大祸。

《国家事务》的作者讲述了艾瑟尔河（the Ijssel）岸上的那场伏击，讲「那个英格兰人为逃命在漂流的浮冰间泅渡」。看来他登上对岸时已筋疲力尽，人也冻得半僵，在河岸上躺了好一阵，神志半昏半醒。不过很快，他警觉到还可能有新的伏击，便重新上路，在近旁寻到一间无人居住的茅屋，钻进去暂避风头。天一擦黑，他便动身返回聚特芬（Zutphen），去警告马凯（Marquet）不要再前进。执政（the Stadtholder）的全部计划显然已被某个内奸泄露给了德·贝格——那内奸是谁，第欧根尼当时不可能猜不到——如今再想从原定的任何一个渡口横渡艾瑟尔河，都纯属疯狂。

===Original===
To reach Zutphen at this juncture meant for the undaunted adventurer two leagues and more to traverse, and with clothes frozen hard to the skin. But he did reach Zutphen in time, and with the assistance of Marquet, then evolved the plan of an advance into Gelderland by effecting the crossing of the Ijssel as far north as Apeldoorn, and then striking across the Veluwe either to Amersfoort or to Ede, threatening de Berg’s advance, and possibly effecting a junction with the Stadtholder’s main army.

After this understanding with Marquet, Diogenes then proceeded to Arnheim, where the garrison could now only be warned to hold the city at all costs until assistance could be sent.

In the meantime, de Berg’s troops were swarming everywhere. The Englishman could only proceed by night, had to hide by day on the Veluwe as best he could. Hence much delay. More than once he was on the point of capture, but succeeded eventually in reaching Arnheim.

===Chinese===
在这个当口要赶到聚特芬，对这位无所畏惧的冒险家来说，意味着要走上两里格（league）开外的路，而且身上的衣裳冻得硬邦邦，紧贴着皮肉。但他还是及时赶到了聚特芬，并在马凯的协助下，定出了进兵海尔德兰的方略：在远至北面的阿珀尔多伦（Apeldoorn）一带渡过艾瑟尔河，随后横穿费吕韦（the Veluwe），直插阿默斯福特或埃德（Ede），威胁德·贝格的推进，并尽可能与执政的主力会师。

与马凯计议停当之后，第欧根尼随即赶往阿纳姆（Arnheim），那里的守军如今只能受到这样的告诫：不计一切代价守住城池，等候援兵。

与此同时，德·贝格的部队正四处蜂拥。「那个英格兰人」只能昼伏夜行，白天在费吕韦上勉力找地方藏身，因此耽搁了不少时日。他不止一次险些被俘，但最终还是抵达了阿纳姆。

===Original===
Here he saw Coorne, who was in command of the small garrison, assured him of coming relief, and made him swear not to surrender the city, since the Stadtholder would soon be on his way with strong reinforcements. Thence to Nijmegen on the same errand. A more easy journey this, seeing that Isembourg had not begun his advance from Kleve. After that, De Keysere and Wageningen.

Van Aitzema says that it was between Nijmegen and Wageningen that “the Englishman,” lurking in a thicket of scrub, overheard some talk of how the Stadtholder was to be waylaid and captured on his return to camp from Amersfoort. This fact the chronicler must have learned at first hand. By this time the forces of de Berg were spreading over Gelderland. “The Englishman” gathered that the Archduchess’s plans were to leave Isembourg’s army to deal with Arnheim and Nijmegen for the present, whilst de Berg was to march on Ede, and, if possible, push on as far as Amersfoort. But as to how the coup against the Stadtholder was to be effected, he could not ascertain. At the time he did not know that his Highness intended to visit Amersfoort again. But for him, that little city where Gilda dwelt was just now the hub of the universe, and thank Heaven his errand was now accomplished, all his Highness’s orders executed, and he was free to go to his young wife as fast as his own endurance and Spanish vedettes would allow.

===Chinese===
在阿纳姆，他见到了统率那支小小守军的库尔讷（Coorne），向他保证救援必至，并让他起誓绝不献城，因为执政不久便将率领强有力的增援启程前来。随后，他又为着同一桩使命赶赴奈梅亨（Nijmegen）。这一程走得较为顺当，因为伊森堡（Isembourg）还没有从克莱沃（Kleve）出兵推进。那之后，便是德·凯泽雷（De Keysere）与瓦赫宁恩（Wageningen）之行。

范·艾特泽马说，正是在奈梅亨与瓦赫宁恩之间，「那个英格兰人」潜伏在一丛灌木里，无意中听到了几句话，说的是执政从阿默斯福特回营的路上将如何遭到拦路劫持。这件事，编年史家想必是得自第一手。到这时，德·贝格的兵力已在海尔德兰四处铺开。「那个英格兰人」揣度出大公夫人（the Archduchess）的方略：暂且留下伊森堡的军队对付阿纳姆与奈梅亨，而由德·贝格进军埃德，可能的话再一路推进到阿默斯福特。至于对执政的这记突袭究竟要如何下手，他却探查不出。当时他还不知道殿下有意再度亲临阿默斯福特。而对他来说，吉尔达（Gilda）栖身的那座小城，此刻便是宇宙的中心；感谢上苍，他的差事如今已经办完，殿下的各项命令都已执行妥当，他可以放手赶去见他年轻的新娘了——能走多快，就看他的脚力与西班牙人的哨探肯容他多快了。

===Original===
This meant another tramp across open country, which by this time was overrun with enemy troops. Fugitives from Ede were everywhere to be seen. “The Spaniards. They are on us!” rang from end to end of the invaded province, and the echo of that dismal cry must by now have been rolling even as far as Utrecht.

It meant also seeking cover against enemy surprise parties, who threw the daring adventurer more than once out of his course, so that we hear of him once as far south as Rhenen, and then as far east as Doorn. It meant hiding amongst the reeds in the half-frozen marshes, swimming the Rhyn at one point, the Eem at another; it meant days without food and nights without rest. It meant all that, and more in pluck and endurance and determination, to which three qualities in “the Englishman” the worthy chronicler, though ever chary of words, pays ungrudging tribute.

===Chinese===
这意味着又要在旷野间长途跋涉，而此时四乡已遍布敌军。埃德的逃难者四处可见。「西班牙人。他们打过来了！」这声哀号响彻遭受侵略的行省首尾，其回声此刻想必已一路滚到了乌得勒支（Utrecht）。

这也意味着要时时找掩护，防备敌人的突袭队——那些突袭队不止一次把这位大胆的冒险家逼得偏离路线，以至于我们听说，他有一次南窜远至雷嫩（Rhenen），另一次又东逃到了多伦（Doorn）。这意味着藏身于半冻沼泽的芦苇丛中，在有一处泅渡莱茵河（the Rhyn），在另一处泅渡埃姆河（the Eem）；意味着连日无食、彻夜无休。这一切，再加上胆魄、耐力与决心上的更多付出——对「那个英格兰人」的这三种品质，那位惜墨如金的可敬编年史家也毫不吝惜地致以敬意。

===Original===
He reached Amersfoort, as we know, just in time to see the Stadtholder leave the city in the company of the traitor, Nicolaes Beresteyn, and, struck by that same treacherous hand, fell, helpless for a moment, at the very threshold of the burgomaster’s house.

After which began the martyrdom which had ended in such perfect triumph and happiness.

The daring adventurer, left lonely and stricken upon the moorland, did in truth go through an agony of misery and humiliation such as seldom falls to the lot of any man. Indeed, what he did suffer throughout that terrible day, whilst he believed himself to be irretrievably blinded, was never known to anyone save to the two faithful friends who watched lovingly over him. Socrates, after he had accompanied the Stadtholder, returned to sit and watch with Pythagoras beside the man to whom they both clung with such wholehearted devotion.

===Chinese===
我们知道，他赶到阿默斯福特时，恰好来得及望见执政在那叛徒尼古拉斯·贝雷斯坦的随侍下出城而去；随即，又是那只奸恶的手击中了他，他一时动弹不得，正倒在市长（burgomaster）宅邸的门槛边。

自那以后开始的这场受难，最终以那样圆满的胜利与幸福收场。

这位大胆的冒险家，孤零零、带着重伤被撇在荒原上，确实经受了一场痛苦与屈辱的煎熬，其惨烈鲜少落到任何人头上。真的，在那可怕的一整天里，当他相信自己已无可挽回地失明时，他所受的种种罪，除了那两位满怀爱意守护着他的忠实朋友，无人得知。苏格拉底送走执政之后，便回来与毕达哥拉斯一同坐在那个人身旁守望——他们两人都以那样的赤诚忠心追随着他。

### II / 二

===Original===
It was not until late in the night that a faint glimmer of light, coming from the fire which the two caitiffs had managed to kindle as the night was bitterly cold, reached the young soldier’s aching limbs, and seemed to him like a tiny beacon of hope in the blackness of his misery. By the time that the grey dawn broke over the moorland, he had realized that the injury which he had thought irremediable, had only been transient, and that every hour now brought an improvement in his power of vision.

Whereupon, three heads were put together to devise a means for using the Englishman’s supposed blindness to the best advantage. One wise head and two loyal ones, not one of them even remotely acquainted with fear, what finer combination could be found for the eventful undoing of a pack of traitors?

Ede was in the hands of the Spaniards, Amersfoort on the point of sharing the like fate. These facts were sufficiently confirmed by the stray fugitives who wandered homeless and distracted across the moorland, and were in turn interrogated by the three conspirators.

===Chinese===
直到深夜，一缕微光才终于照到这年轻军人酸痛的四肢上——那是两个恶棍因夜寒彻骨而设法生起的一堆火发出来的——在他眼里，那仿佛是他苦难黑暗中一星希望的灯塔。待到灰白的黎明破开荒原的天际，他已经明白：他原以为无可救药的创伤，其实只是一时的，如今他的目力每个钟头都在好转。

于是，三颗脑袋凑到一处，筹谋着如何把这英格兰人「假定的失明」利用到极致。一颗智慧的脑袋，两颗忠诚的脑袋，且没有一颗哪怕远远见识过恐惧——要干成那件惊心动魄的大事、倾覆一伙叛贼，还能寻出比这更妙的搭配么？

埃德已落入西班牙人之手，阿默斯福特也濒临同样的命运。这些情形，由那些无家可归、失魂落魄地流荡于荒原之上的零散逃难者充分证实——三位「同谋」又挨个儿盘问了他们。

===Original===
With the woman he loved inside the invaded city, and with that recreant Stoutenburg in command of the enemy troops there, Diogenes’ first thought was to get into Amersfoort himself at all risks and costs. As for the plan for freeing the town and punishing the miscreants, it was simple enough. To collect a small troop of ruffians from amongst the fugitives on the moorlands and place these under the command of Socrates, was the first move. The second was to send Pythagoras with an urgent message to Marquet to hurry eastward with his army from Apeldoorn, to the relief of Amersfoort, taking on his way the lonely molen on the Veluwe, where an important detachment of enemy troops might be expected to encamp.

The one thing with which Diogenes was, most fortunately amply provided, was money; money which he had by him when first he started out of Amersfoort on the Stadtholder’s errand; money which was needful now to enable Socrates to recruit his small army of ne’er-do-wells and to assist Pythagoras on his embassy to Marquet.

Thereafter Diogenes, feigning blindness and worse, made his way into the presence of the Lord of Stoutenburg, who held Gilda at his mercy and the whole city to ransom for her obedience.

===Chinese===
心爱的女人身陷沦陷之城，而城中敌军又握在那个背信弃义的斯托滕贝格手里——第欧根尼的第一个念头，便是不惜一切风险与代价，亲自潜入阿默斯福特。至于解救该城、惩办那伙恶棍的方略，倒简单得很。第一步，从荒原上的逃难者中间收罗一小队亡命徒，交由苏格拉底统领；第二步，派毕达哥拉斯携急信驰赴马凯，请他率军从阿珀尔多伦火速东进，驰援阿默斯福特，顺路拿下费吕韦上那座孤零零的磨坊（molen）——那里料想会驻下敌军一支重要的分队。

万幸的是，第欧根尼手里唯有一样东西极其充裕，那便是钱——当初奉执政之差初次走出阿默斯福特时，这笔钱就在他身上；如今这笔钱正派得上用场：既可供苏格拉底招募他那支由闲汉泼皮拼成的小小队伍，也可资助毕达哥拉斯的马凯之行。

此后，第欧根尼装作瞎子，还装得比瞎子更不堪，一路来到了斯托滕贝格勋爵（the Lord of Stoutenburg）面前——此人把吉尔达攥在掌心任凭发落，又拿整座城池作抵押，逼她俯首听命。

===Original===
To dangle before the miscreant’s eyes the prospect of capturing the Stadtholder’s person, and thus make himself master of the Netherlands, was the pivot around which the whole plan revolved. The bait could not fail to attract the ambitious cupidity of the traitor, and verisimilitude was given to the story by Socrates’ band of ruffians, whose orders were to spread the news of the Stadtholder’s advance both on Ede and Amersfoort, and to silence effectually any emissaries of Stoutenburg’s who might be sent out to ascertain the truth of these rumours.

We may take it that Socrates and his little troop saw to it that none of these emissaries did return to Amersfoort for the Lord of Stoutenburg marched out of the city at dawn, with his sinister banner flying, with his musketeers, pikemen and lancers, and with Gilda Beresteyn a virtual prisoner in his train.

===Chinese===
在那恶棍眼前晃出擒获执政本人、从而自登尼德兰主宰之位的锦绣前程——整个计策便绕着这根枢轴旋转。这枚饵食必定勾得起那叛徒野心勃勃的贪欲；而苏格拉底那一伙亡命徒又替这套说辞添上了逼真的凭据——他们奉命四下散布执政已同时向埃德与阿默斯福特进兵的消息，并把斯托滕贝格可能派出探查这些流言虚实的密使，一律干净利落地灭口。

我们可以认定，苏格拉底和他那一小队人马确已料理妥帖，那些密使无一回到阿默斯福特——因为斯托滕贝格勋爵天一亮便开拔出城，阴森的旌旗招展，火枪手、长矛兵与长枪骑兵前呼后拥，吉尔达·贝雷斯坦俨然是他行伍中的一名囚徒。

===Original===
That the daring adventurer risked an ignominious death by this carefully laid plan cannot be denied; but he was one of those men who had gambled with life and death since he was a child, who was accustomed to stake his all upon the spin of a coin; and, anyhow, if he failed, death would have been thrice welcome, as the only escape out of untold misery and sorrow.

Chance favoured him in this, that at the last he was left face to face with the burgomaster, to whom he immediately confided everything, and who enabled him to escape out of the house by the service staircase, and thence into the streets, where no one knew him and where he remained all night, effectually concealed as a unit in the midst of the crowd. He actually went out of Amersfoort in the train of Stoutenburg; and whilst his lordship’s troops made a long halt at Barneveld, “the Englishman” continued his way unmolested across the Veluwe to the lonely molen, which was to witness his success and happiness, or the final annihilation of all that made life possible.

===Chinese===
这条精心布下的计策令这位大胆的冒险家冒着身败名裂而死的危险，这一点无可否认；可他本是那种自幼便拿生死当赌注的人，惯于把全部身家押在一枚钱币的旋转上；况且，纵然失手，死亡也是三倍地受欢迎——那是从无尽苦难悲愁中脱身的唯一出路。

命运在此也眷顾了他：最后他被单独留在了市长面前，他当即向市长吐露了全部实情；市长便助他从佣人楼梯逃出宅邸，转入街市——那里无人识得他，他便在人群中藏了整夜，藏得严严实实，就是人堆里毫不起眼的一个。他竟是跟着斯托滕贝格的队伍一道出的阿默斯福特；趁勋爵大人的兵马在巴讷费尔德久久停驻，「那个英格兰人」不受一丝惊扰，径自穿过费吕韦，走向那座孤零零的磨坊——那里将见证他的成功与幸福，或者见证一切使生命尚可忍受的东西尽数毁灭。

### III / 三

===Original===
All this and more, in the matter of detail, hath the meticulous chronicler of the time put conscientiously on record. We must assume that he was able to verify all his facts at source, chiefly through the garrulous offices of “the Englishman’s” two well-known familiars.

What, however, will forever remain unrecorded, save in the book of heroic deeds, is a woman’s perfect loyalty. During those hours and days, full of horror and of dread, Gilda never once wavered in her belief in the man she loved. From the moment when Nicolaes tried to poison her mind against him, and through all the vicissitudes which placed her face to face with what was a mere semblance of her beloved, she had never doubted him, when even the Stadtholder seemed to doubt.

She knew him to be playing a dangerous game﻿—but a game for all that﻿—when first she beheld him, sightless and abject, in the presence of their mutual enemy, and had rested for one brief second against his breast. That his eyes, still dazed by the poisonous fumes, could vaguely discern her face, even though they could not read the expression thereon, she did not know. The fear that he was irremediably blind was the most cruel of all the tortures which she had undergone that night.

===Chinese===
这一切，连同更多的细节，当年那位一丝不苟的编年史家都已尽心尽责地载入史册。我们可以推定，他的种种事实皆能在源头核实——主要得力于「那个英格兰人」那两位出了名的多嘴亲随的饶舌效劳。

然而，有一件事将永远不见于文字记载——除非记在英雄事迹的册页上——那便是一个女人完美的忠贞。在那些充满恐怖与惊惧的时日里，吉尔达对她所爱之人的信念从未有过一丝动摇。从尼古拉斯企图往她心里灌毒、使她厌弃他的那一刻起，历经种种变故——那些变故把她推到当面，面对的不过是她心上人的一副躯壳表象——她始终不曾怀疑过他，哪怕在连执政都似乎起了疑心的时候。

她初次见到他——双目失明、形容卑琐，立在他们共同的仇人面前——并在他胸膛上偎依了短短一瞬的时候，便已知道他在玩一场危险的游戏——但终究只是一场游戏。她并不知道，他那双仍被毒烟熏得迷蒙的眼睛，虽读不出她脸上的神情，却能依稀辨出她的面容。唯恐他已无可救药地失明——那是她那一夜所经受的种种酷刑中最残忍的一种。

===Original===
When her father came to her in the small hours of the morning to tell her that all was well with the beloved of her heart, but that he would have all the need of all her courage and of all her determination to help him to complete his self-imposed task, she realized for the first time how near to actual death the torturing fear had brought her. But from that time forth, she never lost her presence of mind. With marvellous courage she gripped the whole situation and played her role unswervingly until the end.

Everything depended on whether Marquet reached the molen before the Lord of Stoutenburg, or his captains suspected that anything was wrong. True, Pythagoras had brought back the news that he had met the loyal commander at Apeldoorn, and that the latter, despite the fact that he and his troops intend to take there a well-earned rest, had immediately given the order to march. But, even so, the future of the Netherlands and of her Stadtholder, as well as the fate of the gallant Englishman and his beloved wife, lay in the hands of God.

===Chinese===
凌晨时分，父亲来到她房里，告诉她：她心爱的人一切安好，只是他要办成那桩自己揽下的差事，正极需要她全部的勇气和全部的决心来相助。直到这时，她才头一回明白，那番折磨人的恐惧曾把她逼到离真正的死亡多么近的地方。但自那一刻起，她再没有失过方寸。她以惊人的胆识把整个局面牢牢攥在手里，毫不动摇地把她的角色一直演到了终场。

一切都系于两点：马凯能否赶在斯托滕贝格勋爵之前抵达磨坊；勋爵的众队长又会不会起疑，察觉出任何不对。诚然，毕达哥拉斯带回了消息，说他在阿珀尔多伦见到了那位忠诚的指挥官，后者尽管原打算让部队在那里歇一个应得的脚，还是当即下达了开拔的军令。但即便如此，尼德兰与其执政的前途，连同那位英勇的英格兰人与他爱妻的命运，都仍握在上帝手里。

===Original===
One hour before dusk Marquet’s vedettes first came in contact with the outposts of Beresteyn’s encampment in the gorge below the molen. There was a brief struggle, fierce on both sides, until the main body of Marquet’s army, four thousand strong, appeared on the eastern heights above the gorge.

Whilst the Lord of Stoutenburg ran round and round the narrow space wherein he was a hunted prisoner, trying to escape that shameful death which threatened him at the hand of two humble justiciaries, his few hundred men were falling like butchered beasts beneath the pike-thrusts and musket shots of Marquet’s trained troops.

Nicolaes Beresteyn was the first to fall.

It was better so. Dishonour so complete could be only wiped out by death.

When, a day or two later, after Marquet had driven the Spaniards out of Amersfoort, the burgomaster heard the news of the death of his only son. He murmured an humble and brokenhearted: “Thank God!”

===Chinese===
黄昏前一小时，马凯的哨探首次同贝雷斯坦营地的前哨接上了火——那营地扎在磨坊下方的山峡里。双方短暂交锋，俱都凶猛，直到马凯麾下四千大军的主力现身于山峡东侧的高地上。

当斯托滕贝格勋爵在那方狭小的空间里团团乱转——他已是瓮中被围猎的囚徒——拼命想躲开那两个卑微的执法者将要加诸他身的耻辱死亡时，他那几百号部下正像被屠宰的牲畜一般，倒在马凯训练有素的部队的长矛捅刺与火枪射击之下。

尼古拉斯·贝雷斯坦第一个倒下。

这样倒更好。如此彻底的耻辱，唯有死才能洗刷。

一两天后，马凯已把西班牙人逐出了阿默斯福特，市长听到了独子死去的消息。他谦卑而心碎地喃喃道：「感谢上帝！」

## XVII The Only World / XVII 唯一的世界

### I / 一

===Original===
Out there, in the lonely molen on the Veluwe, Gilda had remained for a while, half numb with nerve strain, suffering from the reaction after the terrible excitement of the past few hours. Presently her old serving-woman came to her, still raging with choler at the outrage committed against her person by those two abominable rascallions.

With great volubility, she explained to her mistress that they had fallen on her unawares when first she had been sent downstairs by his lordship﻿—whom may God punish!﻿—They had bound and gagged her, and then told her quite cheerfully that this was an act of friendship on their part, to save her from a worse fate and from the temptation of talking when she should remain silent.

She had been thrust into a dark angle of the millhouse, from whence she could see absolutely nothing, and where she had lain all this while, entirely helpless, hearing that awful din which had been going on outside, expecting to be murdered in cold blood at any moment, and tortured with fear as to what was happening to her mistress. Only a few moments ago, the two ruffians had reappeared, running helter-skelter down the steps and thence out through the door into the open. Fortunately, one of them, conscience-stricken no doubt, had thought, before fleeing, to release her from her bonds.

===Chinese===
在外头，费吕韦（the Veluwe）那座孤零零的磨坊（molen）里，吉尔达（Gilda）又逗留了一阵——神经绷得太久，人已半是麻木，正经受着过去几个钟头那场可怕的激动过后的反应。不多时，她的老侍女来到她身边，兀自满腔怒火，恨着那两个可恶的恶棍无赖加诸她本人的暴行。

她滔滔不绝地向女主人诉说：起初她奉勋爵大人之命——愿上帝惩罚他！——头一回被打发下楼时，他们便冷不丁朝她扑了上来；他们把她捆了个结实，堵上了嘴，过后还满脸快活地告诉她，说这是他们俩的一番友爱之举，好救她免遭更坏的命运，也免得她在该缄口的时候，受不住多嘴的诱惑。

她被推进磨坊屋里一处黑黢黢的角落，从那儿什么也绝对瞧不见；方才这一路，她便一直躺在那里，全然无计可施，耳听着外头那阵可怕的喧嚣闹个不停，时时刻刻只当自己就要遭人冷血杀害，心里又为女主人此刻的境遇惊惧煎熬。就在片刻之前，那两个恶徒重新露了面，跌跌撞撞地奔下台阶，夺门而出，跑进旷野里去了。所幸其中一个——想必是良心发现——临逃之前倒还想到替她松了绑。

===Original===
Maria was stupid, uncomprehending and garrulous; but she was loyal, and had a warm and ample bosom, whereon a tired and aching head could find a little rest.

Gilda, her body still shaken by hysterical sobs, her teeth chattering, her senses reeling with the horror of all that she had gone through, found some measure of comfort in the old woman’s ministrations. A mugful of wine, left over from the midday meal, helped her to regain command over her nerves. Holding her young mistress in her arms, Maria, crooning like a mother over her baby, rocked the half-inert young form into some semblance of sleep.

===Chinese===
玛丽亚（Maria）人笨，懵懵懂懂，又絮叨个没完；可她忠心，还有一副温暖宽厚的胸怀，好叫一颗疲惫作痛的脑袋在那里头觅得少许安歇。

吉尔达的身子还叫歇斯底里的抽泣震得发颤，牙齿咯咯打战，神志也仍为她经历过的那一切恐怖晕眩摇晃，却在老妇人的一番照拂里得了几分慰藉。中午饭剩下的一大杯酒下了肚，帮她重新稳住了自己的神经。玛丽亚把年轻的女主人搂在怀里，像母亲哄着婴孩一般低低哼着，将那半瘫软的年轻身子摇啊摇的，摇出了几分入睡的模样。

### II / 二

===Original===
And here Diogenes found her a couple of hours later, curled up like a tired child in the arms of the old woman.

He came up on tiptoe, carrying a lantern, for now it was quite dark. This he placed on the floor, and then, with infinite caution, he slid into Maria’s place and took the beloved form into his own strong arms.

She scarcely moved, just opened her eyes for a second or two, and then nestled closer against his shoulder, with a little sigh, half of weariness, but wholly of content.

===Chinese===
两个钟头之后，第欧根尼（Diogenes）就在这里寻着了她——她蜷在老妇人怀里，活像个困倦透了的孩子。

他提着一盏提灯，蹑手蹑脚地走上前来——这会儿天已经全黑了。他把灯搁在地板上，然后怀着万分的谨慎，悄然挪进玛丽亚空出的位置，把那心爱的身躯接进了自己坚实的臂弯里。

她几乎没动弹，只睁开眼睛一两秒钟，随即往他的肩头偎得更紧了些，轻轻叹出一口气——半是困倦，却整个儿是心满意足。

===Original===
She was just dead-tired after all she had gone through, and now she slept just like a baby in his arms; whilst he was as happy as it is possible for any human being to be, for she was safe and well, and nothing could part her from him now. He was satisfied to watch her as she slept, her dear face against his breast, her soft breath coming and going with perfect evenness through her parted lips.

Once he stooped and kissed her, and then she woke, put her arms around his neck, and both forgot for the time being that there was another world save that of Love.

===Chinese===
经过这一切之后，她实在是累乏到了极点，此刻便像婴孩一样睡在他怀里；而他呢，已然快活到了一个人所能有的极致——因为她平安无恙，如今再没有什么能把她从他身边夺走了。他心满意足地望着她安睡，望着她那可爱的脸庞贴在自己胸膛上，柔和的气息从微启的双唇间匀匀地一进一出。

有那么一回，他俯下身去吻她；她便醒了，伸出双臂搂住他的脖颈。一时间，两人都忘了，除了爱的世界，这世上还有别的世界。
